# Campaign Findings

One entry per invariant violation or notable observation. Reported as one
batch at the end of the campaign; the operator prioritizes.

Severity: **critical** (data loss / silent corruption / security),
**major** (incorrect visible behavior, unbounded recovery),
**minor** (diagnosability, docs gap, cosmetic).

Entry template:

    ## <scenario-id> — <one-line title>
    - **Invariant:** I<n> (<name>)
    - **Severity:** critical | major | minor
    - **Repro:** <commands / probe name>
    - **Observed:** <what happened, with log/query excerpts>
    - **Expected:** <what the docs/spec promise>
    - **Notes:** <fix ideas, related known issues>

---

## W0-1 — PgBouncer transaction pooling causes silent leader-election split-brain and duplicate schedule fires
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w0-1-pgbouncer-txn-pooling.md`, probe section (observation steps 1-5). Baseline (no overlay) confirmed healthy first: single leader (`controller2`), 2 runs in ~90s from a 1/min schedule, SSE streaming live `tick N` events. Probe stacked `../edgecase/compose/pgbouncer.override.yaml` (all three controllers' `UNIFIED_DB_DSN` routed through `pgbouncer` in `POOL_MODE: transaction`) onto the same compose file and repeated the job+schedule apply.
- **Observed:** All three controllers independently logged `"scheduler became leader"` within an 11-minute window, with no corresponding "lost leadership"/"stepped down" message ever logged by any of them:
  ```
  controller1-1 | {"time":"2026-07-29T01:47:53.589Z","level":"INFO","msg":"scheduler became leader"}
  controller2-1 | {"time":"2026-07-29T01:57:06.415Z","level":"INFO","msg":"scheduler became leader"}
  controller3-1 | {"time":"2026-07-29T01:57:09.477Z","level":"INFO","msg":"scheduler became leader"}
  controller1-1 | {"time":"2026-07-29T02:05:18.889Z","level":"INFO","msg":"scheduler became leader"}   # re-claimed, no restart/kill involved
  ```
  From the moment all three had claimed leadership, `edge-every-minute` fired **3x per cron tick** instead of once (`GET /api/v1/runs?jobName=edge-tick` sample, one minute's worth):
  ```
  createdAt: 2026-07-29T01:57:06.418461Z  triggeredBy: schedule:edge-every-minute
  createdAt: 2026-07-29T01:57:09.479238Z  triggeredBy: schedule:edge-every-minute
  createdAt: 2026-07-29T01:57:54.540495Z  triggeredBy: schedule:edge-every-minute
  ```
  Run count grew from 0 to 26 in ~10 minutes (expected-healthy ~10). No `advisory`/`lock`/`unlock`-related log line ever appeared on any controller — `grep -i "advisory\|lock\b\|unlock"` over all three logs matched nothing but the routine `"postgres pool configuration"` line. `pg_locks` (queried directly against `postgres`, bypassing the pooler) showed a stable set of 6 granted advisory locks held by only 3 distinct Postgres backend pids (68, 69, 282) throughout (observed interactively; raw query output not captured — re-capture when this scenario is promoted to a permanent test) — i.e. PgBouncer's small pool of *real* server-side sessions is shared/multiplexed across the three controllers' logical connections, so a lock "held" by one controller's client connection is actually attached to a shared backend session another controller's transaction can just as legitimately borrow next. That is the mechanism behind the split-brain: it produces zero Postgres-level lock errors because, from Postgres's point of view, every acquire/release is happening on a real, valid session — the pooling layer is what silently reassigns "the same" session identity to different application-level leaders. Restarting `controller1` (kill+recreate) and waiting 60s did not strand any lock (still 6 granted, no dead/orphaned pids) and did not stop the split-brain — it simply re-joined and re-claimed leadership on its own 90s later, unprompted. Killing the apparent last-elected leader (`controller3`) did not halt scheduling either: runs kept accumulating afterward (26 -> 27 -> 28 -> 28 -> 30 -> 30 over ~106s) at roughly the reduced (2-leader) duplicate rate, driven by the two survivors.
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md` requires session pooling and states that transaction pooling "breaks advisory locks and NOTIFY." The expected failure mode per the docs would be lock breakage; what we could not tell from the docs alone is whether that breakage is loud (errors an operator could alert on) or silent. It is the latter: no error, warning, or lock-contention message of any kind was logged anywhere in the stack; the only externally visible symptom is the schedule firing 2-3x too often and multiple `"scheduler became leader"` lines across replicas that a log-scraping alert would need to specifically watch for.
- **Notes:** Recommend the docs/runbook explicitly call out that the failure signature is "duplicate `scheduler became leader"` lines across >1 controller ID" and "run count exceeding schedule cadence," since there is no explicit split-brain or lock-loss error to grep for. A cheap mitigating fix: emit a "scheduler lost leadership" (or periodic leader-lock renewal failure) log line distinct from "became leader," so flapping is visible without cross-referencing run timestamps. Also worth an invariant check (I1) at the run-creation layer: reject/dedupe schedule-triggered runs for the same schedule tick within a short window, independent of leader-election correctness, as defense in depth.

## W0-1 — Controllers crash-loop with no DB-connect retry at startup; pgbouncer overlay reliably triggers it via a boot race
- **Invariant:** I5 (bounded recovery — diagnosability)
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** Same probe as above, `docker compose -f docker-compose.ha.yaml -f ../edgecase/compose/pgbouncer.override.yaml up -d --build` cold start (not a steady-state observation — this happened on the very first `up`).
- **Observed:** Two of three controllers exited immediately on first boot because they queried Postgres (via `pgbouncer`) before PgBouncer's listener/DNS record was fully live, and neither retries:
  ```
  controller2-1 | {"level":"ERROR","msg":"store init","error":"ping postgres api pool: failed to connect ... connection refused"}
  controller3-1 | {"level":"ERROR","msg":"store init","error":"ping postgres api pool: ... lookup pgbouncer on 127.0.0.11:53: no such host"}
  ```
  Both processes then exited(1) rather than retrying, which in turn made `nginx` (whose upstream config lists all three controllers by DNS name at startup) fail to start at all, since Docker's embedded DNS had already dropped the dead containers' records:
  ```
  nginx-1 | 2026/07/29 01:47:53 [emerg] 1#1: host not found in upstream "controller2:8080" in /etc/nginx/nginx.conf:7
  ```
  This cascaded to `agent-enroll` looping on `dial tcp: lookup nginx ...: no such host` for its full 60-retry budget and exiting with `controller never became ready for agent1`, leaving the compose `up -d --build` command itself reporting a failure even though `postgres`, `pgbouncer`, and `controller1` were all healthy. Manually re-running `docker compose up -d controller2 controller3 nginx` (letting them retry against an already-warm pgbouncer) then `up -d agent-enroll agent1 agent2` recovered the stack fully with no further errors.
- **Expected:** A transient dependency race at boot (a sibling container's DNS/listener not yet ready) is the kind of condition a clustered controller should retry through, not crash-exit on — especially since `postgres`/`pgbouncer` already gate `depends_on: condition: service_healthy` for the DB but there is no equivalent healthcheck/retry for the controller-to-pgbouncer hop or for nginx-to-controller DNS resolution.
- **Notes:** Not unique to transaction-pooling mode — a plain extra network hop (any TCP proxy in front of Postgres) would likely reproduce it — but the pgbouncer overlay is what surfaced it here since it's the first scenario in this campaign to insert a proxy between controller and Postgres. Recommend either a startup retry loop on `store init` (bounded, with backoff) or a `depends_on: condition: service_healthy` + healthcheck on the `pgbouncer` service itself so controllers don't race it.

## W0-1 — SSE/NOTIFY log streaming keeps working under transaction pooling, despite the docs' blanket claim
- **Invariant:** I5 (bounded recovery — diagnosability / docs accuracy)
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** Same probe stack (pgbouncer overlay active, split-brain already underway). Attached to a currently-running run's SSE endpoint: `curl -N --max-time 15 "localhost:18080/api/v1/runs/cc467bdc-8853-48b8-9973-b4e5ec609ada/events" -H "Authorization: Bearer ha-admin-token"`.
- **Observed:** Log lines streamed live and in order, same shape as the healthy baseline — no stall, no gap, no reconnect needed:
  ```
  data: {"type":"log","seq":541,"stepIndex":0,"stream":"stdout","line":"tick 1","timestamp":"2026-07-29T02:02:57.998135Z"}
  data: {"type":"log","seq":542,"stepIndex":0,"stream":"stdout","line":"tick 2","timestamp":"2026-07-29T02:02:57.998135Z"}
  data: {"type":"log","seq":543,"stepIndex":0,"stream":"stdout","line":"tick 3","timestamp":"2026-07-29T02:02:59.99823Z"}
  ...
  data: {"type":"log","seq":552,"stepIndex":0,"stream":"stdout","line":"tick 12","timestamp":"2026-07-29T02:03:08.005493Z"}
  ```
  (full capture not committed; `seq` increments continuously, `curl` received bytes throughout the 15s window rather than blocking until timeout.)
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md` states transaction pooling "breaks advisory locks and NOTIFY," which reads as a blanket claim covering both mechanisms. Advisory locks did break (see the split-brain entry above), but NOTIFY-driven SSE delivery did not visibly break in this run — an operator reading the docs literally would expect log streaming to also stall or go silent under this misconfiguration, and it didn't.
- **Notes:** Either the controller's SSE/log-delivery path doesn't actually depend on LISTEN/NOTIFY the way the docs imply (e.g. it falls back to polling, or the connection happened to land on a session that kept a live LISTEN registration for the run's duration), or NOTIFY breakage requires conditions this run didn't hit (e.g. longer runs, more concurrent listeners, a pooler reconnect mid-stream). Recommend either correcting the docs to be precise about which mechanism breaks (advisory locks, confirmed) versus which is untested/unconfirmed (NOTIFY), or a follow-up scenario specifically designed to stress LISTEN/NOTIFY under transaction pooling (e.g. a long-running job with a mid-run pgbouncer server-connection cycle) before asserting NOTIFY breakage as fact.

## W0-2 — TZ-divergent replicas disagree on whether a schedule is due for the same physical instant
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `go test -tags edgeprobe ./internal/controller -run TestEdgeProbe_TZDivergentLeaders -v` (`internal/controller/edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go`).
- **Observed:** A UTC-context check and a JST-context check of the exact same schedule (`"0 9 * * *"`) at the exact same physical instant (00:05 UTC vs 00:06 UTC — one minute apart, but the schedule's own `next`/due computation is evaluated against `now.In(<replica's Location>)`) reach opposite due/not-due conclusions:
  ```
  edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:134: UTC-leader fired=0, JST-takeover fired=1, last_fired_at=2026-07-29 09:00:00 +0900 JST — TZ divergence causes skip/dup? utc=0 jst=1
  ```
  The UTC-context leader sees the schedule as ~9h from due (`fired=0`); the JST-context leader, checking essentially the same moment, sees `09:06 JST` and fires it (`fired=1`). Nothing in the run-creation path detects or reconciles this — whichever replica happens to be leader when it evaluates `now` in its own Location wins, silently.
- **Expected:** Cron due-ness should be a property of the physical instant plus the schedule definition, not of which `time.Location` happens to be attached to the `now` value a given leader passes into `dsl.NextCronTime`/`checkAndFireSchedules`. A schedule should fire exactly once per intended occurrence regardless of which replica (or which replica's local/env TZ) is currently leader.
- **Notes:** This is the most concerning result from the W0-2 batch (per Task 5's analysis) because it is a live architectural landmine, not a rare boundary condition: any HA deployment where replica hosts/containers don't have `TZ` pinned identically (or where `now` is constructed via `time.Now()` on hosts with different local zones, or passed through `.In(...)` inconsistently anywhere in the call chain) is exposed to this on every leader handoff, producing either a duplicate fire (if the new leader's Location makes an already-fired occurrence look due again) or a skipped fire (the reverse). Recommend: (1) normalize `now` to UTC at every entry point that computes cron due-ness, never trusting an arbitrary Location on the caller-supplied timestamp; (2) add a startup/health check that asserts all controller replicas report the same effective TZ; (3) treat this as a strong argument for pinning `TZ=UTC` in the HA compose/deployment manifests explicitly rather than leaving it to host defaults.

## W0-2 — Catch-up window boundary: an occurrence missed by one cron interval (5 min) past the 1h window is silently and permanently dropped
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), I5 (bounded recovery — diagnosability)
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `go test -tags edgeprobe ./internal/controller -run TestEdgeProbe_CatchupWindowBoundary -v` (`internal/controller/edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go`).
- **Observed:** The closed lower edge of the catch-up window (`next == now-60m` exactly) correctly fires:
  ```
  edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:81: boundary next==now-1h: fired=1 last_fired_at=2026-07-29 20:00:00 +0900 JST
  ```
  But one cron interval (5 minutes, the `*/5` schedule's own granularity) further out (`next == now-65m`, from a `last_fired_at` seeded at `now-66m` = 19:54 JST), the occurrence is dropped with zero signal of any kind:
  ```
  edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:89: just outside window: new-runs=0 (silent skip? true) last_fired_at=2026-07-29 19:55:00 +0900 JST
  ```
  No run is created, and there is no log line, error, warning, or metric distinguishing "occurrence intentionally outside catch-up window, dropped" from "nothing was due yet." `last_fired_at` IS advanced to the skipped occurrence (19:55 JST, i.e. the `next` value) — `internal/controller/scheduler.go:197-201` takes this path unconditionally when `next < windowStart`, so the run is dropped but the bookkeeping still moves on. The only trace that an occurrence was silently lost is the absence of a run row a human would have to know to go looking for; nothing distinguishes this `last_fired_at` advance from the one that happens on a normal fire.
- **Expected:** A 1-hour catch-up window is a reasonable, presumably intentional bound (I5 — "bounded recovery"), but a schedule occurrence that falls outside it should be a detectable event, not indistinguishable from normal steady-state operation. An operator recovering from an outage that lasted a few minutes longer than the window gets no indication that a tick was lost.
- **Notes:** Argued as **major** primarily because the missed occurrence is *permanently* lost, not merely delayed — no catch-up ever happens for it, unlike the backward-clock-step or DST-gap entries below where the affected occurrence is late but still eventually fires. A scheduled run that was due and never executes at all is incorrect behavior in its own right (silent data loss against I1, run accounting), independent of how loud or quiet the failure path is. The total silence (no log line, error, warning, or metric distinguishing this from steady-state) is an aggravating factor that compounds the severity — it means operators cannot even detect after the fact that an occurrence was dropped — but is not itself the primary basis for the major rating; on diagnosability alone this would only be minor per this file's own severity rubric. Not critical, because it's a single bounded occurrence rather than ongoing/systemic corruption, and the window's existence is itself a documented, intentional design choice. Per a reading of `scheduler.go:197` (`next < windowStart → too old to fire`), this silent-drop path applies to any occurrence outside the window by any margin, not just the 5-minute case the probe exercised — that broader claim is inferred from the code, not directly demonstrated by this probe. Recommend at minimum a `WARN`-level log line (`"schedule occurrence outside catch-up window, skipped"`, with schedule ID and how far outside) whenever this path is taken.

## W0-2 — observation: backward clock step causes a bounded, self-healing stall equal to the skew magnitude
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** `go test -tags edgeprobe ./internal/controller -run TestEdgeProbe_BackwardClockStep -v` (`internal/controller/edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go`).
- **Observed:**
  ```
  edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:151: with future last_fired_at: fired=0 (every-minute schedule silent until wall clock catches up: true)
  ```
  A `last_fired_at` stamped 10 minutes in the future (e.g. from a leader with a fast/skewed clock) causes an every-minute schedule to stop firing when checked against the real, correct `now`. This is bounded and self-healing: because the scheduler only fires when the computed `next` time is after `last_fired_at`, the schedule resumes on its own — no operator action, no restart, no manual reset — as soon as real wall-clock `now` catches up past the erroneous future stamp. The stall duration equals the skew magnitude (~11 minutes for the tested 10-minute-ahead skew, given the every-minute cadence); no occurrence is permanently lost, and the schedule is back to normal cadence immediately once the clock passes the bad timestamp. Structurally this is the same recovery shape as the DST-gap entry below (single bounded delay, automatic resumption, no data loss), not an unbounded one.
- **Expected:** A future `last_fired_at` from clock skew is a fault condition; recovery should be bounded once the underlying skew is no longer ahead of real time. Observed behavior matches this: the delay is bounded by, and directly proportional to, the size of the skew, with no manual intervention required.
- **Notes:** Rated minor rather than major, consistent with the DST-gap entry's rating for the same recovery shape. Caveat/extrapolation beyond what was directly observed: the probe only exercised a 10-minute skew, and nothing in the observed logic caps how large a skew can be written to `last_fired_at` in the first place — a much larger erroneous future stamp (from a badly corrupted clock, NTP misconfiguration, or a bug elsewhere writing a bogus timestamp) would plausibly produce a proportionally longer stall of the same bounded/self-healing shape, but potentially large in wall-clock terms, and it would be silent and undetectable for its entire duration (no metric or log flags "schedule N minutes overdue due to a future last_fired_at"). This is inferred, not tested — the probe does not establish how large a skew the system will tolerate before writing `last_fired_at`, if there is any limit at all. If large-skew robustness is a real operational concern, recommend clamping/rejecting `last_fired_at` writes that are ahead of the server's own `now` (`pg.UpdateScheduleLastFiredAt`) as defense in depth, and/or a health check comparing each schedule's expected next-fire time to wall clock — a hardening suggestion, not evidence of an unbounded defect as tested.

## W0-2 — DST spring-forward gap: a daily schedule silently skips the transition day instead of firing near the gap
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** `go test -tags edgeprobe ./internal/controller -run TestEdgeProbe_DSTGap -v` (`internal/controller/edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go`).
- **Observed:**
  ```
  edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:46: DST gap: next('30 2 * * *') after 2026-03-08 01:00:00 -0500 EST = 2026-03-09 02:30:00 -0400 EDT (skipped? true)
  ```
  `02:30` does not exist on 2026-03-08 in `America/New_York` (clocks jump 02:00 -> 03:00). `dsl.NextCronTime` does not fast-forward to the nearest valid time on the same day (e.g. 03:30) or fire once at the gap boundary — it skips the entire remainder of March 8th and returns March 9th's `02:30 EDT` instead, a ~24h delay versus the schedule's nominal daily cadence.
- **Expected:** Not explicitly documented behavior either way, but an operator reading `"30 2 * * *"` as "runs daily at 2:30am" would not expect a full calendar day to be skipped on the two-per-year DST transition dates without any indication.
- **Notes:** Kept at minor rather than major because the recovery is bounded and predictable (exactly one missed occurrence, resuming automatically the next day at the correct time — this is standard, if under-documented, behavior for cron libraries using wall-clock arithmetic over a `Location`), and it affects at most one occurrence twice a year for daily-or-coarser schedules in DST-observing zones. Worth a one-line callout in scheduling docs ("schedules that land in the spring-forward gap skip to the next valid occurrence rather than firing early/late on the same day") so this isn't a support-ticket surprise.

## W0-2 — observations (no violation)
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `go test -tags edgeprobe ./internal/controller -run 'TestEdgeProbe_DSTFold|TestEdgeProbe_BacklogDrainRate' -v` (`internal/controller/edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go`).
- **Observed:**
  - **DST fold** (ambiguous wall-clock hour repeats): calling `NextCronTime("30 1 * * *", ...)` twice in sequence across the November fold returns two distinct instants exactly one real hour apart, both labeled "01:30" in local wall-clock terms:
    ```
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:60: DST fold: first=2026-11-01 01:30:00 -0400 EDT (2026-11-01 05:30:00 +0000 UTC) second=2026-11-01 01:30:00 -0500 EST (2026-11-01 06:30:00 +0000 UTC) — fires twice on the fold night? true
    ```
    This is the mirror image of the spring-forward gap finding above (ambiguous instant walked through as two firings instead of a nonexistent instant being skipped) and is standard `time.Location`-based wall-clock cron semantics, not a scheduler-specific defect — noted here as a known, low-probability (twice a year, one hour, DST-observing zones only) duplicate-fire source for the record.
  - **Backlog drain rate**: with a 30-minute backlog (6 due `*/5` occurrences queued) and 8 sequential calls to `checkAndFireSchedules`, the total run count climbs exactly 1-per-call for the first 6 calls and then flattens once the backlog is exhausted:
    ```
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 1: total runs=1
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 2: total runs=2
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 3: total runs=3
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 4: total runs=4
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 5: total runs=5
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 6: total runs=6
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 7: total runs=6
    edgeprobe_scheduler_test.go:107: call 8: total runs=6
    ```
    Confirms the drain rate is exactly one backlog occurrence per `checkAndFireSchedules` call, matching the brief's prediction — at the production cadence of one check per minute, a 30-minute scheduler outage takes ~6 real minutes to fully drain. This is expected, bounded, and self-terminating behavior; no defect.
- **Expected:** Both match the behavior predicted going into the probe design (DST fold as documented cron-library wall-clock semantics; drain rate as a direct consequence of the one-occurrence-per-call design). Included for completeness/record, not as findings.
- **Notes:** No action needed. DST fold could be paired with the spring-forward gap callout in scheduling docs if that doc gets written.

## Checkpoint: W0 complete
- Scenarios run: W0-1 (pgbouncer transaction-pooling overlay, 3 findings entries), W0-2 (6 scheduler clock/TZ boundary probes: DST gap, DST fold, catch-up window boundary, backlog drain rate, TZ-divergent leaders, backward clock step)
- Classification rule: a FINDINGS entry is a **violation** when observed behavior contradicts an invariant (I1-I7) or a documented contract; it is an **observation** when behavior matched expectations but reveals a risk. This rule is applied consistently — later waves should count entries the same way.
- Violations: 6 (W0-1 split-brain [major], W0-1 crash-loop-no-retry [minor], W0-1 NOTIFY-docs-overclaim [minor], W0-2 TZ-divergent leaders [major], W0-2 catch-up-window silent-and-permanent skip [major], W0-2 DST-gap day-skip [minor]) — 3 from W0-1 + 3 from W0-2 (3 major: W0-1 split-brain, W0-2 TZ-divergent, W0-2 catch-up-window; 3 minor: W0-1 crash-loop-no-retry, W0-1 NOTIFY-docs-overclaim, W0-2 DST-gap). Observations: 2 (W0-2 backward-clock-step bounded stall — its own Expected confirms behavior matched expectations, no invariant violated; W0-2 combined DST-fold + backlog-drain-rate entry).
- Impact on later waves: The `edgeprobe`-tagged, build-tag-gated unit-test probe pattern established here (drive `checkAndFireSchedules`/`NextCronTime` directly with constructed `now` values, since libfaketime/time-namespace clock injection is not viable against static Go binaries) is directly reusable for W2's boundary scenarios (W2-6 scheduler-crash-mid-fire, W2-8 approval-timeout clock skew) without needing container-level clock manipulation. The compose-overlay pattern from W0-1 (stack a `.override.yaml` onto the base HA compose file to inject a single infra misconfiguration, diff against a confirmed-healthy baseline) is validated and ready for W3's cache/restore-race scenarios. Also carrying forward from W0-1: the docs' NOTIFY-breaks-under-transaction-pooling claim needs a caveat (advisory locks confirmed broken; NOTIFY/SSE was not observed to break in this probe) — flagged as a docs-follow-up candidate, not yet filed.

## W1-1 — observation: all-controller restart during a 300s run survives with zero log loss, zero duplication, and no run-completion delay
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), I4 (log completeness/ordering), I5 (bounded recovery)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-1-all-controller-restart.md`. Baseline gate confirmed first (single leader `controller1`, `readyz` 200). Applied `edge-longrun` (`test/edgecase/workloads/longrun.payload.json`, 300 `tick N` lines at ~1/s), triggered run `39d87dcb-113c-4c80-aa43-c454734e8c4d` (claimed by `agent2`), confirmed live SSE delivery through `tick 54`, then `../edgecase/tools/inject.sh kill-hard` against `controller1`, `controller2`, `controller3` in sequence (04:54:07-04:54:09 UTC), confirmed `readyz` returned 502 for the full outage window, then `docker compose -f docker-compose.ha.yaml up -d controller1 controller2 controller3` (04:55:28-04:55:30 UTC).
- **Capture provenance (read before relying on any timestamp below).** Unlike the W1-5 and W1-6 entries further down, which open with explicit raw-evidence lists and are fully re-derivable, this scenario saved only two artifacts to the scratchpad: `w1/trigger-response.json` (the trigger response: run id `39d87dcb-...`, `createdAt 2026-07-29T04:53:04.683801Z`) and `w1/run-logs.json` (the complete 300-entry log body), plus the `w1/analyze.py` that parses it. No controller logs, no agent logs and no `readyz` poll output were captured for W1-1.
  - **Fully substantiated by a saved artifact — do not caveat these:** the entire **Line accounting (I4)** bullet below. `w1/run-logs.json` + `w1/analyze.py` re-derive it end to end (300 entries, `seq` 1-300 strictly increasing with no gaps or duplicates, `tick 1`..`tick 300` each exactly once, zero lines matching `dropped`). Re-run: `python w1/analyze.py`.
  - **Corroborated but not directly captured:** the run's completion instant. The saved body's last entry is `tick 300` at `04:58:05.166947Z`, which is consistent with the `Succeeded`/`finish` timestamps quoted below (`04:58:05.740150Z` / `04:58:05.742860762Z`) but is not the same measurement. Note also that the saved body's *first* entry is `tick 1` at `04:53:07.099056Z`, so the "step began executing at `04:53:05.08Z`" figure below is from a live read, not from the artifact.
  - **Observed live, raw output not captured to scratchpad:** everything keyed to controller or agent logs or to `readyz` polling — specifically the outage window `04:54:09Z`->`04:55:30Z` and the "`502` on every check" claim, `controller1`'s leader re-election at `04:55:30.339751164Z`, the first post-recovery `logs/bulk` flush at `04:55:33.144550456Z`, the quoted `agent2` 502 error lines, the `logs/stats` response, the SSE replay, the `GET /runs?jobName=edge-longrun` listing, and the `controller3` leadership-blip tally in the leader-election note. None of these can be re-checked from the scratchpad; a re-runner disputing them would have to re-execute. This does not affect the entry's headline (zero log loss / zero duplication), which rests entirely on the artifact-backed line accounting.
- **Observed:**
  - **Outage window:** all three controllers down 04:54:09Z -> 04:55:30Z (~81s); `readyz` polled every 10s during this window returned `502` on every check, never `200`.
  - **Agent behavior during outage:** `agent2`'s claim/heartbeat/cancel-poller calls failed continuously with `http 502` (e.g. `{"msg":"claim","error":"http 502: response omitted","detached":true}`, `{"msg":"agent heartbeat failed", ...}`), but the agent process did not crash or abandon the run — it kept retrying in a loop, consistent with `LogPusher`'s buffer-and-retry design (`internal/agent/runner.go`).
  - **Recovery timing (I5):** `docker compose up -d controller1 controller2 controller3` was issued and completed within the `04:55:28Z`-`04:55:30Z` window. `controller1` re-acquired leadership (`"scheduler became leader"`) at `04:55:30.339751164Z` — within ~0.3-2.3s of that `up -d` window (leader-election-from-restart figure). The first successful post-recovery agent report (a `logs/bulk` flush, HTTP 204) landed on `controller1` at `04:55:33.144550456Z` — ~2.8s after leader re-election, and ~3-5s after the `up -d` window (first-report-from-restart figure). No unbounded stall.
  - **Run completion timing:** the run reached `Succeeded` at `04:58:05.740150Z` (per the `finish` call at `04:58:05.742860762Z` on `controller2`). The run was triggered at `04:53:04.683801Z` and the step began executing at `04:53:05.08Z`; 300 one-second ticks from that start predicts completion at ~`04:58:05Z` — i.e. the measured completion time matches the *uninterrupted* baseline expectation almost exactly. The ~81s controller outage added no visible delay to wall-clock run completion: the agent kept executing the local shell step throughout the outage and only the *reporting* was delayed/buffered, not the *execution*.
  - **Line accounting (I4):** fetched via `GET /api/v1/runs/<id>/logs?after=0` (`handleTailLogs` — sufficient in one call since total lines were well under the 1000-line page size; no trim occurred so the archive route was not needed) and cross-checked against `GET /api/v1/runs/<id>/logs/stats`, which reported `{"count":300,"minSeq":1,"maxSeq":300}`. Parsed the full log body: exactly 300 lines, all `tick 1`..`tick 300`, `seq` 1-300 strictly increasing with no gaps and no duplicates, tick numbers 1-300 with zero missing and zero extra values. No `[N log line(s) dropped: controller unreachable]` marker appeared anywhere in the log.
  - **SSE re-attach:** `curl -N .../events` against the completed run replayed the full 300-line backlog in order (`seq 1` = `tick 1` ... ) and closed cleanly; no stall, no error.
  - **I1 (run accounting):** `GET /api/v1/runs?jobName=edge-longrun` after the probe shows exactly one run, `Succeeded` — no duplicate or phantom runs from the outage/recovery.
  - **Leader-election note (not a violation):** during the kill sequence, `controller3` briefly claimed leadership at `04:54:07.528423572Z` — i.e. in the sub-second gap between `kill-hard controller1` (the prior leader) and the subsequent `kill-hard controller3` commands, before `controller3` itself was killed moments later. This is sequential failover (old leader's Postgres advisory-lock session dying releases the lock, a survivor grabs it), not overlapping/split-brain leadership like the W0-1 pgbouncer finding: `grep -c "scheduler became leader"` afterward shows `controller1: 2, controller3: 1` with no time overlap between any two claims, and no duplicate runs resulted. `grep -i "lock\|advisory"` across all controller logs matched nothing (as in the healthy W0-1 baseline).
- **Expected:** Per the failsafe-audit's LogPusher pending-buffer design (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-16-resilience-2.md`), a bounded controller outage should be absorbed by client-side buffering with zero log loss and the run should still reach a terminal state once connectivity returns; this scenario's outage (~81s, ~300 tiny log lines, well under the 1MiB `maxPendingBytes` cap) is squarely inside that bound, and behavior matched exactly: zero loss, zero duplication, correctly ordered logs, one run record, bounded recovery.
- **Notes:** This run's outage produced far too little buffered data (a few KB of `tick N` lines) to exercise the `droppedLines`/marker eviction path (`appendPendingLocked`, 1MiB cap) — that path remains untested by this probe; a follow-up scenario with either a much longer outage or a much higher-throughput log producer would be needed to observe the drop-marker mechanism in action (tracked as a gap, not a defect). Also worth carrying into W2: the sub-second `controller3` leadership blip during the kill sequence is an artifact of injecting the three `kill-hard` calls sequentially rather than simultaneously — a scenario that kills all three truly atomically (e.g. `docker kill` in one shell command against all three at once) would remove even that transient handover and might be a cleaner variant if W2 wants a strictly zero-leader-transition version of this probe.

## W1-2 — Postgres outage overlapping a step's natural completion silently and permanently drops the tail of that step's log lines, with no drop marker
- **Invariant:** I4 (log completeness/ordering)
- **Severity:** major
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-2-postgres-restart.md`, injection variant B (`kill-soft postgres`, escalated to a forced `kill-hard` — see the companion minor entry below for why). Baseline gate confirmed first (single leader `controller1`, `readyz` 200). Applied `edge-longrun`, triggered run `698a9af9-a10f-4d02-ab1e-0c6246c8846d` (claimed by `agent2`), confirmed live SSE delivery through `tick 66`, then forced postgres down with `SIGKILL` at `05:25:09Z` (after the `kill-soft`/`SIGTERM` at `05:22:00Z` left Postgres in an indefinite "smart shutdown" limbo — see below), then `docker compose -f docker-compose.ha.yaml up -d postgres` at `05:26:17-05:26:18Z`.
- **Capture provenance — read this before disputing the severity, and note precisely which half of the argument is re-checkable.** W1-2 saved two artifacts, both log bodies: `w1/w1-2/run-b-logs.json` (this variant) and `w1/w1-2/run-a-logs.json` (variant A, the observation entry below). No controller logs, no agent logs, no Postgres logs and no `readyz` poll output were captured for either variant.
  - **Fully substantiated by `w1/w1-2/run-b-logs.json` — the loss itself:** 262 entries; `seq` exactly `326`..`587` contiguous; `tick` `1`..`262` each present exactly once; `tick 263`-`300` absent; zero entries matching `dropped`. That is the finding's central fact and it is independently re-derivable.
  - **Substantiated for two of the three root-cause timestamps:** the last successfully-delivered line is `tick 262` stamped `05:25:08.389803Z` in that file, which is the `05:25:08.39Z` "last successful flush" anchor below; and the step-start anchor is likewise in the file (`tick 1` at `05:20:48.346983Z`), from which the `~05:25:47Z` natural step end is arithmetic, not an unrecorded observation. (Both are agent-side line timestamps, i.e. when the line was *written*, not when the batch was *accepted* — close enough to anchor the argument, but not literally a flush-acknowledgement record.)
  - **NOT substantiated — the third timestamp, and it is the one that closes the argument:** `readyz` returning `200` at `05:26:23Z` was observed live and never redirected to a file, as were the `SIGKILL` at `05:25:09Z`, the `SIGTERM` at `05:22:00Z`, and every Postgres/controller/agent log line quoted in this scenario's other entries. The arithmetic that establishes the outage *outlasted* the bounded 5s `Flush` — step ends `~05:25:47Z`, DB still unreachable until `05:26:23Z`, so the whole ~5s budget elapsed while down — therefore rests on one figure a fix author cannot re-check. **Stated plainly so a triager is not surprised by it.**
  - **This does not soften the severity, and the entry is deliberately left at major.** The code mechanism is independently verifiable from source with no rig at all: `Flush` (`internal/agent/runner.go:335-353`) is bounded by `context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), 5*time.Second)` at `runner.go:343` and does up to 3 retries 1s apart, `stopAutoFlush()` runs *before* it (`backend_host.go:382-386`), nothing calls `Flush` on that `LogPusher` again, and `droppedLines` — the marker's sole trigger — is incremented only by `appendPendingLocked`'s cap-eviction path, which a 38-line batch never reaches. Given a permanently-lost tail with no marker (artifact-backed) and that mechanism (source-backed), the unrecoverable `readyz` timestamp affects only how tidily the two are joined, not whether the defect is real.
- **Observed:** `GET /api/v1/runs/<id>/logs/stats` after the run reached `Succeeded` reported `{"count":262,"minSeq":326,"maxSeq":587}` — 300 expected, only 262 delivered. A Python pass over the full captured log (`GET /logs?after=0`) confirmed: `seq` 326-587 fully contiguous (no gaps, no duplicates) and `tick` numbers 1-262 each present exactly once, but **`tick` 263-300 (38 lines) are permanently absent** — not delayed, not duplicated elsewhere, just gone. `grep -o '\[.*log line(s) dropped: controller unreachable\]'` over the captured log file matched nothing: the documented drop-marker mechanism never fired for this loss — and, as the root-cause trace below shows, its precondition (`droppedLines > 0`) was never even met, not merely unlucky timing on an otherwise-eligible marker.
  Root cause, traced through `internal/agent/runner.go` and `internal/agent/backend_host.go`:
  - `hostBackend.StepLogWriters`'s `finish` closure (`backend_host.go:382-386`) calls `stopAutoFlush()` **first** (permanently killing the 2s auto-flush ticker for that `LogPusher`), **then** `Flush(finishCtx)`.
  - `LogPusher.Flush` (`runner.go:335-353`) does one `flushLocked` attempt and, if anything is still pending, up to 3 further retries 1s apart bounded by an **independent, hardcoded 5-second timeout context** (`context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), 5*time.Second)`, `runner.go:343`) — this is a fixed, short, one-shot retry budget, structurally different from the **unbounded** `retryUntilSuccess` backoff (`retry.go:24-53`, doubling 1s -> 30s cap, retried forever until success or ctx-done) that `ReportStep`/`SetRunOutputs`/`FinishRun` use (`orchestrator.go:666`, `781`, `787`). **Caveat (see the W1-5 major entry below):** "unbounded... retried forever" describes `retryUntilSuccess`'s behavior on 5xx/transport errors only — the same loop gives up immediately, with no retry at all, on any `HTTPError` with `StatusCode < 500` (`retry.go:33-36`); read this entry and the W1-5 4xx-permanent-abandonment finding together.
  - The last successful flush for this run landed at `05:25:08.39Z` (covering `tick 261`-`262`) — 1s before the forced kill. Every 2s auto-flush attempt for `tick 263` onward then failed (DB down) and queued into `LogPusher.pending` via `appendPendingLocked`; the batch is tiny (38 short lines, nowhere near the 1MiB `maxPendingBytes` cap) so the drop-oldest/marker eviction path never triggers — the lines are sitting safely in memory, not evicted.
  - The step's local shell loop (`for i in $(seq 1 300); do echo "tick $i"; sleep 1; done`) finishes on its own regardless of DB reachability (~`05:25:47Z` by the 300x1s-tick arithmetic from the `05:20:47Z` start, consistent with the last successfully-flushed timestamp), at which point `finishLogs(attemptCtx)` runs, stopping the ticker and calling the bounded `Flush`. Postgres was still down for another ~30s past that point (readyz didn't return 200 until `05:26:23Z`), so `Flush`'s entire ~5s retry budget elapsed while still unreachable, and it returned with 38 lines still in `p.pending` — and nothing ever calls `Flush`/`flushPendingLocked` on that `LogPusher` instance again. The lines, and the fact that they were dropped, are both gone for good; the run nonetheless correctly reports `Succeeded` (`ReportStep`/`FinishRun`'s unbounded retry eventually got through — see the companion I5 entry below), so nothing about the run's terminal status hints that its log is incomplete.
- **Expected:** `docs/troubleshooting.md`'s documented behavior for a `LogPusher` under sustained controller/DB unreachability is bounded local buffering with a `[N log line(s) dropped: controller unreachable]` marker on cap overflow — i.e. loss is meant to be loud and to only happen once >1MiB of a single step's output is buffered during an outage. What actually happened is a **different, silent** loss path: a comparatively brief (~74s) outage that happens to still be ongoing at the exact moment a step finishes locally can drop the entire unflushed tail with no marker at all, regardless of how little data was buffered, because the step-end `Flush` gets only a fixed ~5s exit-time retry rather than the same unbounded-retry treatment given to run/step status reporting.
- **Notes:** This is a distinct mechanism from the (correctly-documented, marker-emitting) 1MiB pending-cap eviction path noted as untested-but-present in the W1-1 entry above and in the prior agent-failsafe audit's F9 finding (`unified-cd-audit-findings` memory: "agent LogPusher 1MiB silent drop") — F9 is about the cap; this is about the **step-end bounded retry window**, which can lose data at any size, including far below the cap, as long as the outage outlasts ~5-9s and is still ongoing when the step naturally finishes. W1-1's outage (all three controllers, ~81s) never hit this path because it started and ended entirely *before* the step's natural completion, so the 2s ticker had many chances to drain `pending` well before `finish()` ever ran. This scenario's variant B outage happened to overlap the step's tail by construction (the forced kill landed ~38s before local completion). Recommend either: (a) give the step-end `Flush` the same unbounded `retryUntilSuccess` treatment as `ReportStep`/`FinishRun` (bounded only by a much longer ceiling, or by the same `stepCtx`/process-exit horizon), or (b) if a bounded exit-time retry is intentional (e.g. to avoid indefinitely blocking process shutdown), at minimum increment `droppedLines` (or an equivalent one-shot log/metric) for whatever remains in `pending` when `Flush` gives up — today the existing `[N line(s) dropped]` marker can't help here even in principle, since it fires only when `droppedLines > 0` (set exclusively by `appendPendingLocked`'s cap-eviction path) *and* a later successful flush occurs; this loss satisfies neither condition — the lines are abandoned in `p.pending` without ever being evicted, and no later flush call happens at all for that `LogPusher` instance.

## W1-2 — observation: `kill-soft` (SIGTERM) does not actually take Postgres down while the controllers hold pooled connections open; Postgres enters an indefinite "smart shutdown" limbo instead
- **Invariant:** I5 (bounded recovery — mechanism validity)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms standard Postgres signal semantics, not a unified-cd defect, but invalidates the runbook's planned hard-vs-soft latency comparison as originally written)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-2-postgres-restart.md`, injection variant B, first attempt: `../edgecase/tools/inject.sh kill-soft postgres` (`docker compose kill -s SIGTERM postgres`) at `05:22:00Z`.
- **Capture provenance:** observed live; raw output not captured to scratchpad. Every element of this entry — the Postgres `FATAL: the database system is shutting down` lines, the ten `readyz` 200 checks across `05:22:07Z`-`05:24:25Z`, `docker compose ps postgres` showing `Up (unhealthy)`, and `pg_isready` reporting `rejecting connections` — was read interactively and never redirected to a file. W1-2's only saved artifacts are the two run log bodies under `w1/w1-2/`, which have no bearing on this entry. Low stakes: nothing here is a defect claim against unified-cd (the behavior is documented PostgreSQL Smart Shutdown semantics, reproducible from first principles against any pooled client), and the runbook caveat it produced stands on the mechanism rather than on these timestamps.
- **Observed:** Postgres's own log shows it immediately began rejecting *new* connections (`FATAL: the database system is shutting down`, first occurrence `05:22:05.662Z`, repeating every ~2s indefinitely), matching PostgreSQL's standard SIGTERM ("Smart Shutdown") semantics: refuse new sessions, wait for existing ones to end on their own before the postmaster exits. But all three controllers' `/readyz` kept returning `200 OK` continuously for the entire observed window (`05:22:07Z` through `05:24:25Z`, 10 checks, every check 200 on all three) — because `readyz`'s `store.Ping(pingCtx)` reused an already-established, idle pooled connection, and Smart Shutdown does not forcibly close those. `docker compose ps postgres` showed the container `Up ... (unhealthy)` the whole time (health check failing on new-connection refusal) but never exiting, and `pg_isready` reported `rejecting connections` (not `no response`/down) — the postmaster was alive and waiting indefinitely for the controllers' idle pooled sessions to close, which they never do on their own. After ~3 minutes with no progress, the outage was escalated to a forced `SIGKILL` (`05:25:09Z`) to actually bring Postgres down and let the scenario proceed (documented above as part of the major I4 finding's repro).
- **Expected:** The runbook (`test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-2-postgres-restart.md`) anticipated `kill-soft` producing a comparable-but-gentler outage to `kill-hard`, so hard-vs-soft controller reconnect latency could be compared directly. That assumption doesn't hold: with a connection pool that holds idle sessions open indefinitely (as unified-cd's controller does — no idle-connection eviction was observed to kick in within several minutes), a plain `SIGTERM` to Postgres is **not equivalent to an outage at all** from the application's point of view, for as long as the pool's existing connections remain valid. This is standard, documented PostgreSQL behavior (Smart Shutdown), not a unified-cd bug, but it means "compare hard-kill vs soft-kill controller reconnect latency" isn't answerable by directly killing `postgres` with `SIGTERM` in this compose setup without also forcing new-connection-only traffic (e.g. killing/recreating the controllers' pooled connections, or using `pg_ctl stop -m fast` distinctions, or waiting substantially longer / actively closing controller connections) — a caveat worth folding into the runbook.
- **Notes:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-2-postgres-restart.md`'s variant B section has been updated with a caveat block covering this exact behavior (SIGTERM will not bring postgres down while pooled connections stay open; escalate to `kill-hard` as this probe did) so the runbook stays re-runnable without re-discovering the same 3-minute stall. For a true "clean shutdown" comparison in a future run, either (a) reduce the controller's DB pool idle-connection lifetime so Smart Shutdown can complete in a reasonable window, or (b) use `pg_ctl stop -m immediate`/`SIGQUIT` inside the postgres container instead of a container-level `SIGTERM`, which forces existing backends to terminate rather than waiting for them.

## W1-2 — observation: run-completion reporting lags DB recovery by the remainder of an already-in-flight exponential backoff wait, even though the DB and readyz are both back
- **Invariant:** I5 (bounded recovery)
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** Same run as above (`698a9af9-a10f-4d02-ab1e-0c6246c8846d`), variant B, `agent2` logs.
- **Capture provenance:** observed live; raw output not captured to scratchpad. **This entry is entirely unsupported by any saved artifact** — the six `"retrying after failure"` warnings and their `nextWait` doubling (`05:26:14.04Z` through `05:26:47.58Z`), the `readyz` 200 at `05:26:23Z`, and the run's `Succeeded` `updatedAt` at `05:27:19.60Z` were all read interactively, and `w1/w1-2/run-b-logs.json` (this run's log body) ends at `tick 262` and says nothing about any of them. The ~56s figure is therefore not re-checkable. It is also the softest claim in this scenario — an observation, no invariant violated, and the underlying mechanism (`retryUntilSuccess`'s doubling schedule capped by `RetryMaxWait=30s`, `internal/agent/retry.go:24-53`) is verifiable from source without the rig; only the specific 56s sample depends on the lost capture.
- **Observed:** `agent2`'s `FinishRun` call (via the unbounded `retryUntilSuccess` in `orchestrator.go:787`) logged six consecutive `"retrying after failure"` warnings with a doubling `nextWait`: `1s` (`05:26:14.04Z`), `2s` (`05:26:17.58Z`), `4s` (`05:26:19.58Z`), `8s` (`05:26:23.58Z`), `16s` (`05:26:31.58Z`), `32s` (`05:26:47.58Z`, capped per `retry.go`'s `RetryMaxWait=30s`). `readyz` on all three controllers had already returned to `200` by `05:26:23Z` — i.e. the DB was reachable again roughly at the same moment as the 4th backoff wait (`8s`) started, with two more waits (`16s`, `32s` = 48s) still to run before the *next* retry attempt fired. The run's `updatedAt`/`Succeeded` timestamp (`05:27:19.60Z`) lines up exactly with `05:26:47.58Z + 32s`, confirming the retry loop is what gated completion, not a further DB problem. Net effect: the run's status took **~56s longer** to flip to `Succeeded` than the DB's own recovery time, purely because the backoff schedule that started while the DB was still down isn't interrupted or shortened once the DB comes back — the loop just waits out its already-scheduled full delay before trying again.
- **Expected:** Per I5 (bounded recovery), this is still bounded — `RetryMaxWait` caps any single wait at 30s, so the loop cannot stall indefinitely — but an operator watching this specific run would see the DB/controllers healthy well before the run itself reflects that, which is a real (if capped) gap between "dependency recovered" and "system reflects recovery." Not a violation of any documented promise (nothing promises retries are recovery-aware), but worth recording since it's the direct, measurable cost of an otherwise-reasonable backoff design colliding with bad luck on timing.
- **Notes:** A cheap mitigation, if this lag is judged worth closing, would be a lightweight "wake early" signal (e.g. the retry loop selecting on a short jittered re-check interval rather than sleeping the full backoff, or reacting to a successful `readyz`/heartbeat elsewhere in the same process) rather than committing to the full doubled wait once scheduled. Low priority — the measured worst case here (~56s) is well inside typical operator patience for "an outage just ended," and the underlying cap (30s per wait) already bounds the pathological case.

## W1-2 — observation: hard-kill (`SIGKILL`) variant survives a genuine ~114s Postgres outage cleanly — zero log loss, single clean leader re-election, no visible completion delay
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), I4 (log completeness/ordering), I5 (bounded recovery)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-2-postgres-restart.md`, injection variant A. Baseline gate confirmed (single leader `controller1`, `readyz` 200 on all three). Applied `edge-longrun`, triggered run `78aed211-3464-4d29-8b65-45d9653801e4` (claimed by `agent1`), confirmed live SSE through `tick 68`, then `../edgecase/tools/inject.sh kill-hard postgres` at `05:14:09-05:14:10Z`, then `docker compose -f docker-compose.ha.yaml up -d postgres` at `05:16:03-05:16:04Z`.
- **Capture provenance.** One artifact was saved for this variant: `w1/w1-2/run-a-logs.json`, the complete log body.
  - **Fully substantiated by it:** the whole **Line accounting (I4)** bullet — 300 entries, `tick 1`..`tick 300` each exactly once in order, zero `dropped` matches, and the non-contiguous `seq` (`minSeq=1`, `maxSeq=325`, exactly one gap covering `75`-`99`, re-derivable by set-differencing the `seq` values). The run's completion instant is corroborated too: the last entry is `tick 300` at `05:17:54.792957Z`, consistent with the `Succeeded` `05:17:55.37Z` quoted below.
  - **Observed live, raw output not captured:** the kill/restart timestamps, every `readyz` 503/200 check, `controller1`'s leader re-election at `05:16:06.16Z`, the per-controller leadership tally, the agent 503 error lines, the `logs/stats` response, and the live-SSE `tick 270`-`276` observation. None re-checkable from the scratchpad.
  - One arithmetic note the artifact does bear on: this entry's "execution started ~`05:13:05Z`" is a live read, whereas the saved body's first entry is `tick 1` at `05:12:56.77856Z`. Anchoring to the artifact instead predicts completion at ~`05:17:56Z` against the `05:17:54.79Z` last line — an even tighter fit than the "slightly ahead of a naive estimate" wording below, and it does not change the conclusion (no outage-attributable delay).
- **Observed:**
  - **Outage window:** all three controllers' `/readyz` returned `503` on every check from `05:14:17Z` through `05:15:34Z` (the full observed pre-recovery window); `agent1`/`agent2` logs show continuous `claim`/`agent heartbeat failed`/`cancel poller` failures with `http 503` throughout, with no crash or run abandonment.
  - **Readyz recovery timing (I5):** all three controllers already returned `200` at the first post-recovery check, `05:16:11.52Z` — i.e. **within ~7.5s** of the `up -d postgres` window closing (`05:16:04Z`).
  - **Leader re-election:** `controller1` (the pre-outage leader) re-claimed leadership at `05:16:06.16Z` — **~2s** after `up -d`. Full-campaign tally for this controller across both variants: `controller1: 2` claims (baseline + this recovery), `controller2: 0`, `controller3: 0` for variant A specifically — a single clean re-claim, no thrash, no overlapping claims.
  - **SSE after recovery:** a fresh `curl -N .../events` attached at `05:17:17Z` (after `readyz` confirmed 200) delivered genuinely live, still-ticking lines (`tick 270`-`276` observed arriving in real time, ~2s apart) — confirming LISTEN/NOTIFY resubscribed and is delivering live data, not just replaying a stale backlog.
  - **Run completion timing:** `Succeeded` at `05:17:55.37Z`. Triggered `05:12:54.69Z`, execution started ~`05:13:05Z`; 300 one-second ticks from that start predicts completion ~`05:18:05Z` — the measured completion is, if anything, slightly *ahead* of that naive estimate and shows no added delay attributable to the outage window (`05:14:10Z`-`05:16:11.52Z`, ~121.5s kill-to-first-`200`).
  - **Line accounting (I4):** `GET /logs/stats` reported `{"count":300,"minSeq":1,"maxSeq":325}`. A Python pass over the full log confirmed exactly 300 lines, `tick 1`-`tick 300` each present exactly once in order, zero missing, zero duplicated. No drop marker present. Note: `seq` is **not** contiguous (`minSeq=1`, `maxSeq=325`, a 25-value gap at `75`-`99`) — a server-side `seq`-allocation artifact: `api.LogAppendRequest` carries no client-supplied `seq` field, so `seq` is assigned by the controller at insert time, and the gap corresponds to sequence values burned by insert attempts that failed (or were retried/re-sent as separate attempts) during the outage rather than any client-side numbering. This has **no effect on delivered-line completeness or order**; recorded for completeness, not as a defect, since I4's actual concern (every line delivered exactly once, correctly ordered) held.
  - **I1 (run accounting):** `GET /api/v1/runs?jobName=edge-longrun` shows exactly the two runs from this scenario (this one and the variant-B run below), both `Succeeded`, no duplicates or phantoms.
- **Expected:** Same contract as W1-1: a bounded outage (here, DB-side rather than controller-process-side) should be absorbed with zero log loss and the run should reach `Succeeded` once Postgres is back, per `docs/high-availability.md` §External Dependency Redundancy ("After a PostgreSQL failover, controllers reconnect automatically and leader election re-runs.") and the same `LogPusher` buffering contract exercised in W1-1. Behavior matched exactly for this variant: zero loss, single clean leader re-election, fast `readyz` recovery, live SSE resubscription, no completion delay.
- **Notes:** Outage-duration bookkeeping for this variant, stated as bounds because the exact recovery instant was not sampled: Postgres was down by deliberate hold from `05:14:10Z` (kill complete) to `05:16:04Z` (`up -d postgres` complete) = **~114s**. The app-visible `503` window is known to be **at least ~77s** (first observed `503` `05:14:17Z` through last observed `503` `05:15:34Z`) and **at most ~121.5s** (kill to first observed `200` at `05:16:11.52Z`); the true end lies in the unsampled gap between `05:15:34Z` and `05:16:11.52Z`. (An earlier draft of this entry quoted `~74s` and `~81s` for this variant; both were figures borrowed from other entries — `~74s` is variant B's outage and `~81s` is W1-1's — and neither was ever derived from this variant's own timestamps. Corrected here; the entry's conclusions do not depend on the figure.) This variant's outage ended well before the step's local execution finished (`tick 68` at injection time, ~230 ticks / ~230s still to run), so — consistent with the analysis in the major I4 finding above — it never exercised the step-end bounded-`Flush` loss path; contrast directly with variant B, where the (much later, forced) outage overlapped the step's natural completion and did lose data. The two variants together isolate the real trigger for the I4 finding: not outage duration or hard-vs-soft signal, but whether the outage is still ongoing at the moment a step's log writer is torn down.

## W1-3 — observation, Part A: approval row survives a full-controller restart unchanged, and approve-to-resume latency is bounded by the agent's fixed 3s poll interval
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), I7 (approval/gate integrity)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-3-approval-gate-restart.md`, Part A. Baseline gate confirmed first (single leader `controller3`, `readyz` 200). Applied `edge-approval` (`test/edgecase/workloads/approval.payload.json`), triggered run `3d45ccbc-7b54-491e-ac2c-b2800db6a9df`, polled `GET /runs/<id>/approvals` until the `gate` step (stepIndex 1) showed `Pending` with `createdAt:"2026-07-29T05:49:11.924084Z"`, `timeoutAt:"2026-07-29T05:59:11.923515Z"`. Then `../edgecase/tools/inject.sh kill-hard` against `controller1`, `controller2`, `controller3` in sequence (05:49:57-05:49:59Z), held the outage (`readyz` 502 continuously, five checks 05:50:05Z-05:50:58Z), then `docker compose up -d controller1 controller2 controller3` (05:51:13-05:51:15Z).
- **Observed:**
  - **Outage window:** ~76s (05:49:59Z kill complete -> 05:51:15Z restart complete); `readyz` 502 on every check during the hold, then 502 for four more checks post-restart before flipping to `200` at `05:51:46Z` — a **~31-33s gap between the restart completing and `readyz` answering 200, which is *not* controller boot time**. This branch's own captured controller log contradicts that reading: in `w1/w1-3/controllers.log`, `controller3` logs `{"msg":"controller listening","addr":":8080"}` at `05:51:14.950901078Z` and `{"msg":"scheduler became leader"}` at `05:51:15.153092142Z` — i.e. the process was serving and had won the advisory lock within ~0.2s of the restart window closing (`05:51:15Z`); `controller2` and `controller1` were listening at `05:51:15.129Z` and `05:51:15.112Z`. **Controller process boot plus leader election is ~1-2s here, not ~31s.** The remaining ~31s sits somewhere in the LB/observation path and is unexplained — see the note at the end of this file under "Tooling and asset notes carried forward" for what is and is not established about it. (An earlier version of this line called the figure "controller-boot-to-ready latency, consistent with W1-1/W1-2 figures"; that cross-reference was also wrong — W1-1 reports leader election at +0.3-2.3s and first agent report at +3-5s, which *agrees* with ~1-2s, and W1-2 variant A restarted **Postgres**, not a controller, so it has no controller-boot figure at all.)
  - **Row survival (I7):** `GET /runs/<id>/approvals` immediately after `readyz` returned 200 showed the identical row byte-for-byte on the fields that matter: `stepIndex:1`, `createdAt:"2026-07-29T05:49:11.924084Z"`, `timeoutAt:"2026-07-29T05:59:11.923515Z"`, `status:"Pending"` — no recreation, no deadline shift, no duplicate row. The row is a plain Postgres audit table entry (`internal/store/postgres.go`, `CreatePendingApproval`/`GetApproval`), so this is expected — it is not agent- or controller-memory-resident — but confirms the promise holds under a full-controller-outage stress rather than just steady state.
  - **Approve-to-resume latency:** approved via `POST /runs/<id>/approvals/1 {"decision":"approve"}` at `05:52:16Z` (204); `GetApproval` records `decidedAt:"2026-07-29T05:52:16.765803Z"`, `decidedBy:"env:UNIFIED_TOKEN"`. The `after-gate` log line (from the `after` step, stepIndex 2) landed at `2026-07-29T05:52:17.946326Z` — **1.18s** after the decision was recorded. This is bounded by (and roughly matches) `agent.ApprovalPollInterval = 3 * time.Second` (`internal/agent/agent.go:27`), the fixed interval `WaitForApproval` polls `GetApproval` on — the agent happened to poll shortly after the decision landed rather than waiting the full interval.
  - **Terminal state:** run `Succeeded` at `updatedAt:"2026-07-29T05:52:17.952965Z"`; `GET /runs/<id>/logs?after=0` showed exactly two lines, `before-gate` (seq 1) and `after-gate` (seq 2), in order; `GET /runs/<id>/approvals` final read showed `status:"Approved"`.
- **Expected:** Per I7, a pending approval decision must survive infrastructure churn on the deciding side (the row lives in Postgres, not in any controller's in-memory state) and, once decided, the agent — which is polling independently and was never itself killed — should pick up the decision and resume within one poll interval. Both held exactly as expected.
- **Notes:** No violation. Worth noting for future scenarios: the observed 1.18s latency is a lucky poll-timing sample, not a measured bound — the guaranteed worst case is one full `ApprovalPollInterval` (3s) plus whatever transient poll-error retries were in flight, not the 1.18s figure itself.

## W1-3 — observation, Part B: the two independent timeout clocks (agent-local deadline vs controller-side reaper) fire at very different wall-clock times across a restart, but settle to agreeing terminal states with no I7 disagreement
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting), I7 (approval/gate integrity)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-3-approval-gate-restart.md`, Part B. Triggered a fresh `edge-approval` run `7efd96b1-7a36-4553-b2d3-07474f2a94c0` at `T0="2026-07-29T05:53:17.658965Z"`; gate reached `Pending` with `stepIndex:1`, `createdAt:"2026-07-29T05:53:18.04492Z"`, `timeoutAt:"2026-07-29T06:03:18.044533Z"` (10-minute `timeoutMinutes` from the job spec). Left it un-decided. Killed all three controllers hard at `06:01:58-06:02:01Z` (~T+8m43s, ~76s before `timeoutAt`), held the outage past `timeoutAt` (`readyz` 502 on every check `06:02:10Z` through `06:04:21Z`), restarted at `06:04:38-06:04:39Z` (~T+11m20s, ~80s after `timeoutAt`). `readyz` returned to `200` at `06:05:05Z`.
- **Observed:** The two clocks fired at very different times, exactly per the two-independent-mechanisms design described in the runbook:
  - **Agent clock (fires on schedule, mid-outage, controller-reachability-independent):** `agent1`'s log shows `{"time":"2026-07-29T06:03:18.098984806Z","level":"WARN","msg":"approval timed out","runID":"7efd96b1-7a36-4553-b2d3-07474f2a94c0","step":"gate"}` — **54ms after** the server-stamped `timeoutAt` (`06:03:18.044533Z`), and while all three controllers were still down (restart didn't happen until `06:04:38Z`, 80s later). Immediately preceding it: `{"time":"...098956259Z","msg":"approval poll failed",...,"error":"http 502..."}` (the same-iteration poll failing, confirming the deadline check fired independent of a successful poll) and immediately following it, the run's step-failure path begins its `FinishRun` retry loop: `{"time":"...100525048Z","msg":"retrying after failure","error":"http 502...","nextWait":1000000000}`, then doubling (`2s, 4s, 8s, 16s, 32s`) with waits capped at 32s per `RetryMaxWait` (matching the backoff-lag mechanism recorded in the W1-2 findings above), landing on a scheduled attempt at `06:04:53.119629539Z + 32s ≈ 06:05:25.12s` — which is exactly when the run's `updatedAt` timestamp lands (see below). The `FinishRun` call is wrapped in the unbounded `retryUntilSuccess` (`orchestrator.go:787`), unlike the single best-effort `ReportStep(Failed)` call for the gate step itself (`orchestrator.go:363-366`, no retry wrapper) — no log line confirms whether that specific step-level report ever landed, but it doesn't need to: the run's terminal status is carried by the retried `FinishRun` call regardless.
  - **Controller clock (fires only after restart, on the reaper's next tick):** `controller2`'s log shows `{"time":"2026-07-29T06:05:39.49764867Z","level":"INFO","msg":"approval reaper: marked timed-out approvals","count":1}` — **2m21s after** the agent's local timeout, and only after controllers had been back up for ~1 minute (readyz 200 at `06:05:05Z`), consistent with `RunApprovalReaper`'s 1-minute tick interval (`approval_reaper.go:26-27`) plus advisory-lock/leader-election bootstrap time after the restart.
  - **Final settled state (I7):** run `status:"Failed"`, `updatedAt:"2026-07-29T06:05:25.12777Z"`; approval row `status:"TimedOut"`, `decidedBy:"system"`, `decidedAt:"2026-07-29T06:05:39.494991Z"`. **The run's terminal `Failed` status was actually written ~14s before the row's `TimedOut` status** — the agent-driven `FinishRun` retry succeeded first (once the DB/controllers were reachable and its already-scheduled 32s backoff wait elapsed), and the controller-side reaper caught up to mark the row a tick later. `GET /runs/<id>/logs?after=0` after settling showed only the single `before-gate` line (seq 3) — no `after-gate` — confirming the run never resumed past the gate. No double-timeout, no conflicting writes, no `Approved` row, no re-decided row: `decidedBy:"system"` (the reaper) is the only actor that ever wrote a decision to this row.
- **Expected:** Per I7, once both clocks have settled the row and the run must agree — they need not settle *simultaneously*, since nothing documents that as a promise, only that the eventual verdicts must not contradict. Observed: `TimedOut` row + `Failed` run is exactly the expected pairing, and it was reached via two genuinely independent mechanisms (agent-local wall-clock deadline vs controller-side leader-only reaper tick) racing across a ~2.5-minute controller outage without ever producing a contradiction, a lost approval, or a double-decided row. **Caveat — this "I7 holds" verdict describes only what was actually executed in this probe (nobody attempted a decision during the gap); it is not a guarantee that the window is safe in general** — see the code-confirmed hazard below, which this probe's own timeline exposes but did not trigger.
- **Caveat (code-confirmed, not executed in this probe — repro lead for a future C11/W2-8 scenario):** during the ~14s+ window this run itself produced — run wrote `Failed` at `06:05:25.128Z` while the approval row still read `Pending` (it wasn't marked `TimedOut` until `06:05:39.495Z`) — the row was **not protected against a decision being accepted**. `handleDecideApproval` (`internal/controller/api_approvals.go:13-54`) performs no run-status lookup anywhere in the handler (no call to a run-fetching method, no join, no status comparison against the run) before calling `s.store.DecideApproval`. `DecideApproval` itself (`internal/store/postgres.go:2417-2428`) guards the `UPDATE` exclusively on the approval row's own status column — `WHERE run_id = $1 AND step_index = $2 AND status = 'Pending'` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2421`) — with no reference to the run's status at all. Consequently, an `approve` (or `reject`) POST landing anywhere in that ~14s+ (or longer, in a slower-recovery case) Pending-row/Failed-run window would satisfy the `status = 'Pending'` guard, return `204`, and write `Approved`/`decidedBy`/`decidedAt` onto a row whose run had *already* terminally `Failed` — producing exactly the C11-class contradiction this campaign is watching for (an `Approved` audit row paired with a `Failed` run), via ordinary API use, no restart or injection required beyond what already reproduces the window (an outage that lets the agent's local timeout fire and `FinishRun` land before the controller-side reaper marks the row). Not filed as a violation here because no such POST was attempted in this probe (Part B's design was "let it sit, do not approve") and the window observed live was a `Pending`/`Failed` pairing, not a produced `Approved`/`Failed` contradiction — but the missing guard is real and code-confirmed, and a scenario that races a `decide` POST against this exact window (widen it deliberately, e.g. by slowing controller recovery, then fire the POST mid-window) is a precise, ready-to-run repro lead for W2-8 or a dedicated C11 probe.
- **Notes:** No violation as executed, but three things worth flagging for a future hardening pass: (1) the missing run-status guard on `DecideApproval` above — the fix, if desired, would be adding a run-status check (either a `JOIN`/subquery against `runs.status` in the `UPDATE ... WHERE`, or a pre-check in the handler) so a decision cannot be written once the run has already reached a terminal status; (2) the step-level `ReportStep(Failed)` call on the approval-timeout path is a single best-effort attempt with no retry wrapper (`orchestrator.go:363-366`), so under an outage that starts before the agent's local deadline fires (as here), that specific step-status write is likely lost even though the run-level `FinishRun` status is not — the *step* table may show the gate step's last-known status as something other than `Failed` (not independently re-verified in this probe; the run-level outcome was the focus per the brief) even though the run itself correctly reports `Failed`; (3) the ~2m21s gap between the agent's local timeout and the controller row catching up is itself a direct instance of the "reaper can only run once a controller is up and has ticked" design already called out in the runbook and is bounded (worst case ≈ outage duration + up to 1 reaper interval), not unbounded, so it does not itself rise to an I5 concern at the durations tested here.

## W1-4 — observation, Race A: a cancel committed ~1s before a full-controller kill survives the outage untouched; the agent detects it one poll interval after recovery, and the step's local process is killed at the exact moment of detection (not before)
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-4-cancel-vs-failover.md`, Race A. Baseline gate confirmed first (single leader `controller1`, `readyz` 200). Applied `edge-longrun`, triggered run `b73f557c-21e0-4ac5-a3de-8cba3c732c6b` (claimed by `agent2`), confirmed live SSE ticking through `tick 20`. Issued the cancel and the triple kill-hard as one compound shell command: `curl -X POST .../cancel ... && inject.sh kill-hard controller1 && inject.sh kill-hard controller2 && inject.sh kill-hard controller3`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Cancel-then-kill timing:** cancel `POST` issued `06:26:05.806Z`, returned `204` immediately, and the three `kill-hard` commands (chained with `&&` on the same 204) completed by `06:26:08.605Z` — the whole cancel-to-kill-complete window was under 3s, with the cancel itself committing essentially instantaneously.
  - **Status survives the kill (I1):** the run's `updatedAt` for the `Cancelled` transition is `2026-07-29T06:26:05.843045Z` — i.e. it landed within ~40ms of the `204` response, well before the first `kill-hard` had even been issued. After a ~76s outage (`readyz` `502` on every check `06:26:16Z`-`06:26:36Z`) and a restart issued at `06:26:53.111Z`-`06:26:54.594Z` (`T_restart`), the very first successful `GET /runs/<id>` after `readyz` flipped to `200` (at `06:27:31.986Z`) already showed `status:"Cancelled"` with the *same* pre-kill `updatedAt` — confirming the recovered controllers read back the committed status rather than deciding it retroactively.
  - **Agent detection latency:** `agent2`'s cancel-poller logged 18 consecutive `"cancel poller: get run failed"` (`http 502`) entries, 5s apart, from `06:26:09.934Z` through `06:27:29.939Z` — i.e. it kept trying and failing throughout the entire outage, exactly matching `CancelPollInterval = 5s`. Its next attempt succeeded and observed `Cancelled`: `{"time":"2026-07-29T06:27:34.941359225Z","level":"INFO","msg":"received cancellation signal from master; interrupting run","runID":"b73f557c-..."}`. Measured from `readyz` returning `200` (`06:27:31.986Z`), detection latency was **~2.96s** — inside one `CancelPollInterval`, as expected. Measured from `T_restart`, it was ~40.3s — **but that span is *not* dominated by controller boot or leader election, contrary to an earlier version of this line.** This race's own captured controller log settles it: in `w1/w1-4/race-a-controllers.log`, `controller3` logs `{"msg":"controller listening","addr":":8080"}` at `06:26:54.274319995Z` and `{"msg":"scheduler became leader"}` at `06:26:54.475259384Z` — ~1.2s and ~1.4s after the restart was issued at `06:26:53.111Z`, and before the restart window even closed (`06:26:54.594Z`); `controller2` and `controller1` were listening at `06:26:54.411Z` and `06:26:54.437Z`. **Controller process boot plus leader election is ~1-2s.** The ~37s that then elapsed before `readyz` answered `200` (`06:27:31.986Z`) is unexplained (see the carry-forward note at the end of this file); the remaining ~3s of the 40.3s is the agent's own `CancelPollInterval`, which is the part this bullet actually measures.
  - **Step process actually killed (I6-adjacent measurement; observed live, raw output not captured to scratchpad):** `GET /runs/<id>/logs/stats` settled at `{"count":120,"minSeq":1,"maxSeq":120}` and stayed there on repeated re-checks; the last delivered line was `"tick 120"` timestamped `06:27:34.941786Z` — essentially the same instant as the detection log line, ~0.4ms after it. No `tick 121` or later ever appeared, and `docker compose exec agent2 ps aux` afterward showed no shell/`tick` process, only the agent binary itself — this `ps aux` output was read interactively in the shell and not redirected to a saved file, so it is not independently re-verifiable from the scratchpad the way the agent-log timestamps above are. **However**, because the step's local shell loop (`for i in $(seq 1 300); do echo "tick $i"; sleep 1; done`) has no dependency on controller reachability, it kept running and ticking through the *entire* window between the committed cancel (`tick` ~20 at cancel time) and detection (`tick 120`) — roughly **100 seconds / 100 ticks of step execution that continued after the run was already authoritatively `Cancelled`**, simply because nothing could reach the agent to tell it to stop. This is the expected shape given the polling design (no push-based cancel channel), not a violation, but is exactly the bounded-but-nonzero "keeps executing post-cancel until the next successful poll" cost the brief asked to be measured.
- **Expected:** Per I1, a committed terminal status must be authoritative and must not be re-decided or lost across an immediately-following outage; per the polling design, the agent is expected to detect the change within one `CancelPollInterval` of regaining connectivity, not sooner (there is no push channel) and not much later. Both held exactly. The extended local-execution tail is an inherent property of poll-based cancellation, not a documented promise of prompt interruption — recorded as a measurement, not a defect.
- **Notes:** Worth carrying into a future scenario: the ~100-tick "zombie execution" window here is a function of *when in the outage* the cancel happened to land relative to the agent's next poll opportunity, not a fixed constant — a cancel that raced a controller kill at a different point in a longer-running step, or against a longer outage, would produce a proportionally longer (but still bounded-by-`CancelPollInterval`-after-recovery) tail. If bounding wall-clock waste during long outages is ever a design goal, this is the concrete number to reference.

## W1-4 — observation, Race A Part 2: an ordinary cancel releases the job-level mutex immediately, no leaked `mutex_holders`/`named_lock_slots` rows (I3)
- **Invariant:** I3 (mutex/lock release integrity)
- **Severity:** minor (observation — confirms designed/expected behavior, no defect)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w1-4-cancel-vs-failover.md`, Race A Part 2. Applied an inline job `edge-mutex-cancel` (`spec.concurrency.mutex: edge-mutex-cancel`, single native step `sleep 60`), triggered run `148f87f4-c18d-4a4c-9a70-ad2bb90c2d86` (claimed by `agent2`), confirmed `Running` and confirmed `mutex_holders` held exactly one row (`mutex_name='edge-mutex-cancel', run_id='148f87f4-...', acquired_at='2026-07-29 06:30:03.492126+00'`). Cancelled via `POST /runs/<id>/cancel` (no controller kill in this part — isolates the mutex-release path from the failover race, which Race A Part 1 already covers for run-status commit).
- **Observed (lock-table queries observed live; raw psql output not captured to scratchpad):** Cancel returned `204`; the run's `GET` settled to `status:"Cancelled"` (`updatedAt:"2026-07-29T06:30:21.630145Z"`). Immediately after, both `SELECT * FROM mutex_holders WHERE mutex_name='edge-mutex-cancel'` and `SELECT * FROM named_lock_slots WHERE run_id='148f87f4-...'` returned `(0 rows)`; unscoped full-table scans of both `mutex_holders` and `named_lock_slots` also returned `(0 rows)` — no leaked lock anywhere in either table.
