Add the **5,083** measured claim-ERROR lines (3,483 + 1,600, `w1-6/derived-counts.txt`), the **140** measured heartbeat+cancel-poll failures (83 + 57, same source), and **≈400** (derived) from agent2's 3 exit-time `Flush` retries (`runner.go:335-353`) each re-offering its ~134 still-pending batches — sum **≈20,448**, against the **20,447** measured on the controllers (`w1-6/metrics.txt`), a difference of **1**. Treated as strong corroboration of the mechanism, not as independent proof; the model has enough free parameters (exact tick alignment, the exit-retry count) that an exact match would be somewhat suspicious, and a difference of 1 out of ~20,000 is well inside that slack.
  - **Shape, not just volume.** The same total volume delivered at a flat rate (a hypothetical "N requests spread evenly over the outage") would be a materially different, and much less interesting, finding than what actually happens: the rate itself climbs — derived **~67 req/s** at the last tick before agent2's step ended (134 requests in the final 2s tick alone) — because the mechanism is self-reinforcing: every tick that fails adds to what every subsequent tick has to resend.
- **Expected:** Nothing in `docs/` bounds retry request volume or rate during a controller-unreachable window — the documented contract (`docs/troubleshooting.md`, `docs/high-availability.md`) covers eventual delivery and the 1MiB drop-marker behavior, not request-count shape. I5's "bounded recovery" is about returning to steady state after a fault ends, which is a different property from "resource cost while the fault is ongoing." Recorded as an observation because it is new information about a risk, not because any invariant or contract was found to be violated by it.
- **Notes:** The result worth carrying into W6 is the amplification shape itself, not the scenario-specific 20,447 figure: **any** step that keeps producing output (however slowly) while its `LogPusher` cannot reach the controller — whether the cause is a permanent revocation (this scenario), a genuine transient 5xx outage (W1-1, W1-2), or an intermediary 4xx (W1-5) — pays a request cost that grows quadratically with how long that condition overlaps the step's remaining runtime, because `flushLocked` resends the whole backlog rather than just the newest batch. W1-1's outage (`FINDINGS.md` above, ~81s against a light `tick`-line load) never demonstrated this because the outage started and ended entirely before the step's natural completion, giving the 2s ticker many chances to drain `pending` well before the step-end `Flush` ever ran — so the quadratic shape was present in principle there too but never got the chance to compound. This scenario's ~269s/~214s spans are what exposed it. **Cross-references, not re-filings:** this sits in the same `LogPusher`/`p.pending` machinery as two already-filed items but is a distinct failure/cost shape from both — the prior agent-failsafe audit's **F9** finding (`unified-cd-audit-findings` memory: "agent LogPusher 1MiB silent drop") is about **data loss at the byte cap**, which this scenario's small batches never approached; the **W1-2 major I4** finding above ("Postgres outage overlapping a step's natural completion silently and permanently drops the tail...") is about **data loss under the bounded 5s step-end retry budget**, which this scenario also reproduced (see the log-loss bullets in the entries above) but is mechanistically the *exit* of the same retry loop this entry is about, not its steady-state cost. This entry adds a third, non-overlapping shape: **no data is lost *by this mechanism*** (every batch keeps being retried, confirmed by the `grep -rc "dropped"` = 0 result above; the 266 + ~214 lines this scenario did lose went to the step-end exit path — the W1-2 major — not to the retry loop described here) — the cost is pure wasted request/query volume, growing without bound as long as the step keeps producing and the outage keeps going. Cheapest fix, in the same spirit as F9/W1-2's recommendations: have `flushLocked` send at most the newest batch per tick (or coalesce all pending batches into a single `AppendLogBulk` call) instead of one HTTP request per historically-accumulated batch — this would flatten the shape to O(t) with no change to eventual-delivery semantics or to the (separately-filed) step-end data-loss behavior.

## Checkpoint: W1 complete
- **Scenarios run:** W1-1 (all-controller restart during a 300s run — 1 entry), W1-2 (Postgres outage, injection variants A and B — 4 entries), W1-3 (approval gate across a full-controller restart, Parts A and B — 2 entries), W1-4 (cancel racing controller failover, Race A / Race A Part 2 / Race B — 4 entries), W1-5 (one-way agent->controller partition + the 4xx-abandonment addendum — 3 entries), W1-6 (credential revocation, Parts A/B/C — 5 entries). **19 entries total = 4 violations + 14 observations + 1 resolved branch-internal asset bug** (the last is neither, and is listed separately below). **W1-7 (C13, AppSource reconciler crash mid apply/prune) was not run**, and was not dropped for time or low yield: the campaign spec itself defers it past W3 (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:80`) because `file://` remotes are rejected by design (`dsl.ValidateGitRepoURL`) and the reconciler's own tests are fully mocked, so the scenario needs a git-over-HTTP server container that is too expensive to bolt onto W1. It remains open and owned by W3-or-later.
- **Classification rule:** unchanged from the W0 checkpoint and applied to every entry above — a FINDINGS entry is a **violation** when observed behavior contradicts an invariant (I1-I7) or a documented contract; it is an **observation** when behavior matched expectations but reveals a risk. Two clarifications W1 had to make explicit.
  - **(i) The "documented contract" limb requires a *published* promise** — an exported API field, a schema column, or a statement in `docs/` — and specifically **does not** include an unexported helper's own doc comment. The W1-6 `lastAuthenticatedAt` entry turns on this.
  - **(ii) W1 needed a third bucket the W0 rule did not anticipate: a defect in the campaign's *own* assets, found and fixed inside the same branch.** That is neither a violation (nothing about the product is implicated, and nothing about it is still true at HEAD) nor an observation (the product's behavior was not what was being observed). Exactly one entry falls here — the W1-4 `spec.mutex` payload bug — and it is reported outside both tallies. A rule of thumb for later waves: if the fix landed in this campaign's own tree and the product is untouched, it is a branch-internal asset bug, not a finding. (An earlier version of this checkpoint counted it as a violation on the reasoning that "a checked-in campaign asset that 400s is a broken promise to the next scenario author." That reasoning is only sound while the asset is still broken; once the branch fixes it before merge, there is no promise left to break.)
  - Entry titles encode the classification: observation entries say so in their title and repeat it in their Severity line; the branch-internal asset entry says so in its title and carries an explicit **Classification** line; entries with neither marker are violations.
- **Violations: 4 — 2 major, 2 minor.** (Plus one entry that is neither a violation nor an observation and is listed separately after the four: the resolved branch-internal asset bug. An earlier version of this checkpoint counted 5 violations by including it; it is neither a product defect nor still true, so it has been removed from the tally rather than downgraded.)
  - **major, I4** — W1-2: a Postgres outage that is still ongoing when a step finishes locally silently and permanently drops that step's unflushed log tail (38 lines here), with no `[N log line(s) dropped: controller unreachable]` marker, because the step-end `Flush` gets a fixed 5s retry budget (`internal/agent/runner.go:343`) instead of the unbounded `retryUntilSuccess` treatment given to `ReportStep`/`FinishRun`. **Independently reproduced once more in W1-6 under a different trigger** (permanent auth rejection rather than a DB outage): W1-6 run 1's step reached its natural end at `12:50:23`, so the step-end teardown genuinely ran and gave up — 266 lines lost, no drop marker, and the two `"permanent error, giving up retry"` lines proving the teardown executed. **W1-6 run 2 is not a third reproduction and an earlier version of this checkpoint wrongly counted it as one:** that process was SIGTERMed mid-step by the Part C restart, ~58s short of its 300 ticks, so the step-end `Flush` was never reached (`w1-6/partC-evidence.txt` shows zero permanent-error lines for `agent1`) — its ~214 undelivered lines died with a killed process, which is ordinary process-kill loss, not this defect. So: one genuine second reproduction, one process kill. Both are corroboration/context, not separate filings.
  - **major, I7 (I1 held)** — W1-5: a transient 4xx minted by an *intermediary* makes `retryUntilSuccess` (`internal/agent/retry.go:33-36`) permanently abandon both the step report and `FinishRun`, so a run whose step actually succeeded is permanently recorded `Failed`. Note the invariant attribution deliberately: I1 held (the run does reach exactly one terminal state), the state it reaches is simply wrong, which is an I7 concern. W1-6 *narrows* the fix rather than widening the finding — a controller-originated 401/403 is legitimately permanent, so the recommended fixes are "distinguish 4xx origin" and "make the abandonment visible controller-side", not "retry all 4xx".
  - **minor, I7 — FIXED ON MAIN BEFORE TRIAGE, do not re-open** (PR #113 / `2c018fe` added `MarkRunStepsInterrupted`, called from `failOrphanedRun`; it landed independently while this wave ran, on a commit later than the one the W1 stack was built from — see the STATUS line on the entry itself). W1-5: a reaped/orphan-failed run's in-flight `step_reports` row stays `Running`, `exit_code` NULL, `ended_at` NULL forever, contradicting the run's own terminal status. Systemic rather than partition-specific: every orphan-fail path funnels through `FinishRun`, which never touches `step_reports`. Reproduced on four runs across W1-5 and W1-6, by two different orphan-fail mechanisms.
  - **minor, published-API/schema contract (I7 cited as closest fit only)** — W1-6: `agent_identities.last_authenticated_at` is an exported API field and a real schema column that no code path ever writes, so `lastAuthenticatedAt` is permanently absent from every identity read, indistinguishable from an identity that has genuinely never authenticated. Filed on the contract limb; the entry itself flags the stricter alternative reading (dead code rather than contradiction).
- **Not counted in either tally — 1 resolved branch-internal asset bug (W1-4).** This branch's own Task 2 (`b112b5c`) created `test/edgecase/workloads/mutex-successor.payload.json` and `sideeffect.payload.json` with a `spec.mutex` shorthand that has never existed in the schema, copied from the campaign plan's own worked snippets (now `plan:166`, `:176`, since corrected) — which contradicted that same plan's correct statement at `plan:19`. Both payloads 400'd on first use. Fixed within the branch in `b3158ce` (both now use `spec.concurrency.mutex`), before any scenario consumed the broken form, and the plan's two snippets have since been corrected as well. Kept as an entry above and called out here so the lesson is not lost, but it is **not** a violation: no product code, schema, or documented contract is implicated, and nothing about it is still true at HEAD. The methodological lesson — a plan's own worked example silently contradicting its own "verified facts" block, catchable only by execution — is the same one carry-forward item 2 records for the revocation premise.
- **Observations: 14 — all minor.** By scenario: W1-1 (1: a full controller restart is absorbed with zero log loss, zero duplication and no completion delay), W1-2 (3: `kill-soft` cannot actually stop Postgres while pooled connections stay open; run-completion lags DB recovery by the remainder of an in-flight backoff, ~56s measured; the hard-kill variant survives cleanly), W1-3 (2: the approval row survives a full-controller restart byte-for-byte and approve-to-resume is bounded by the agent's 3s `ApprovalPollInterval`, 1.18s sampled; the agent-local deadline and the controller-side reaper fire ~2m21s apart yet settle to agreeing terminal states), W1-4 (3: a cancel committed ~1s before a triple kill survives untouched and is detected ~2.96s after `readyz` recovers; an ordinary cancel leaves no `mutex_holders`/`named_lock_slots` residue; a cancel attempted during a live outage fails loudly with a 502 and is safely retriable), W1-5 (1: the reaper releases the mutex ~40.2s before the unfenced agent stops, a successor acquires it in 732ms, and the partitioned run still produces exactly its 120 side effects once each), W1-6 (4: revocation is effectively immediate; restart behavior, deregistration and the revoke-vs-disable status-code split; the de-credentialled-agent rejection firehose; the `LogPusher` quadratic request amplification).
- **Invariant tally for the violations** (as each entry states its own attribution — do not re-merge; totals to the 4 violations above): **I4** 1 (major). **I7** 2 direct (1 major + 1 minor), plus 1 more where I7 is cited only as closest fit behind a published-API-contract primary — 3 entries carrying an I7 citation in total, 1 of which is not primarily an I7 finding. **I1, I2, I3, I5 and I6 recorded zero violations in W1** — each held everywhere it was exercised: I1 in all six scenarios, including across a triple-controller kill and two reaps, I2 with exactly 120 side effects once each under partition, I3 with immediate mutex release on both cancel and reap, I5 with every measured reap inside the documented `staleAfter` 90s + interval 30s bound, and I6 measured-not-judged per spec (40.2s, 162.5s, and a >=120.6s lower bound).

### Cross-wave carry-forward

- **1. W1-3 produced a ready-to-run repro lead for W2-8 (C11). Use it directly rather than re-deriving the window.** `DecideApproval` (`internal/store/postgres.go:2421`) guards its `UPDATE` exclusively on the approval row's own `status = 'Pending'` and never looks at the run's status; `handleDecideApproval` (`internal/controller/api_approvals.go:13-54`) performs no run lookup either. W1-3 Part B produced the window live and measured it: the run's terminal `Failed` was written at `06:05:25.128Z` while the approval row still read `Pending` until the reaper marked it `TimedOut` at `06:05:39.495Z` — **~14s during which an `approve` POST would have satisfied the guard, returned `204`, and written `Approved`/`decidedBy`/`decidedAt` onto an already-`Failed` run.** No POST was attempted in W1-3 (its design was "let it sit"), which is exactly why it is a lead and not a filed violation. W2-8's job is to fire that POST mid-window. The window is produced by an outage that lets the agent's local approval deadline fire and its `FinishRun` land before the controller-side reaper's next 1-minute tick, and it can be widened deliberately by slowing controller recovery; no clock-skew injection is required, which matters because W0-2 established that container-level clock skew is not implementable against static Go binaries. If W2-8 confirms it, it is a C11-class I7 violation — an `Approved` audit row paired with a `Failed` run — reachable through ordinary API use.
- **2. A plan-level factual correction landed this wave, and it is also a methodological finding.** The campaign plan's `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-07-29-edge-case-campaign-w1.md:19` "Verified API facts used throughout (do not re-derive)" block asserted that credential revocation "is not immediate", from which the plan derived a ~45-minute blind window (1h access-token TTL minus the 15-min lazy-refresh lead plus jitter) and instructed W1-6 Part A to *observe* that window in action. W1-6 disproved the inference: `agentAuth` (`internal/controller/agent_auth.go:62-77`) re-reads the credential row from Postgres on **every** agent API request and has no server-side credential cache, so a revoked credential stops being accepted the instant the `UPDATE` commits. Measured agent-observed latency was **4.95s** (`revoke-credentials`) and **1.51s** (`disable`) — both entirely the agent's own poll cadence, not a server-side grace. The TTL constants themselves are real and correctly quoted; they simply govern how often the *client* re-exchanges its refresh token, which is a different quantity from how long a token is *accepted*. All four affected plan sites have been corrected in place (`:19` facts block, `:298` Task 8 Interfaces, `:301` Part A, Task 8 Recording bullet), under a `:296` banner that quotes the original wording and marks the task corrected-after-execution — the correction deliberately preserves rather than erases the fact that the scenario was designed against the false premise, because that is the finding. **The methodological point later waves must carry:** a block explicitly labelled "verified facts — do not re-derive" contained a wrong inference built on correct constants, and it took a scenario designed around that inference to catch it. Waves W2-W6 should treat that block as **claims to check cheaply at the point of use**, not as givens — one grep for where the constant is actually consumed would have caught this before a scenario was designed around it.
- **3. The quadratic request-amplification observation is a direct input to W6, specifically W6-S2.** `LogPusher.flushLocked` (`internal/agent/runner.go:358-366`) re-issues a full `AppendLogBulk` call for **every** batch still sitting in `p.pending` on each 2s auto-flush tick, so tick *k* of a sustained outage costs *k* requests and cumulative volume is triangular — **O(t^2) in outage duration, not O(t)**. Measured and derived here: 20,447 rejections measured controller-side against a model reconciling to ~20,448, a derived **~67 req/s** peak at the last tick before the step ended, against a derived **~8.3 req/s** floor once nothing is left to re-flush. **W6-S2 (log/NOTIFY hotspot) picks this up** — it is already the scenario chartered to measure log throughput and `LogPusher` behavior under sustained load/partition, and the amplification is a per-agent multiplier on exactly that load. W6-S1 (connection pressure) should note it as a load-shape input rather than owning it, and W6-S3 (trigger flood) can use the companion result — a de-credentialled agent is a permanent, un-backed-off ~8.3 req/s source of rejected auth, each rejection a wasted Postgres query — as a cheap sustained load generator. Keep the three `LogPusher` items distinct when triaging: **F9** is data loss at the 1MiB byte cap (never approached in W1); the **W1-2 major** is data loss at the bounded 5s step-end retry exit; **this** is pure wasted request/query volume with no data loss by its own mechanism.
- **4. The `LogPusher` disk-spill design decision remains gated on W6-S2 measurement** (spec §6, `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:201-207`), and W1 has now produced two more data points bearing on it — neither changes the gating, but both change what W6-S2 must measure. (i) The W1-2 **major**: the loss path that actually fired in W1 was not the byte cap at all but the step-end `Flush` budget, which spill alone would not fix — it needs the flush-before-finish barrier that §6 lists as the other half of the candidate. (ii) The **amplification observation**: spilling to disk makes the pending backlog cheaper to *hold* but does nothing about resending it, so a spill design that keeps `flushLocked`'s resend-everything behavior would make the O(t^2) request cost *worse* by allowing far more batches to accumulate. W6-S2 should therefore measure request-count shape alongside drop-marker frequency and lost-line counts, and the spill decision should be taken together with the coalesce-the-backlog fix rather than independently of it.

### Forward questions answered

- **(a) Does W1's reaper/lock-release behavior change W2-3's boundary-timing plan? Yes, in three concrete ways.** W2-3 as specified (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-edge-case-testing-design.md:88`) is "stuck-run reaper boundary timing: heartbeat staleness straddling 90s, claims straddling the 60s grace; lock release on reap".
  1. **The 90s boundary is not observable through wall-clock reap timing at compose level — plan W2-3 as a Level-1 deterministic probe, not a timing race.** W1 observed three real reaps against the documented `staleAfter` 90s + interval 30s: **+92.3s** (W1-5 main probe), **+94.1s** (W1-6 Part C) and **+108.9s** (W1-6 Part A), each stated relative to that run's last successful heartbeat. **The three anchors are not equally solid, which matters because this argument turns on the spread.** W1-6's two are measured: `lastSeenAt` was read directly from `GET /api/v1/agents` and froze at `12:55:06.860418Z` (Part C) and `12:45:51.831889Z` (Part A). W1-5's `12:03:16.55Z` is **inferred**, not measured — a successful heartbeat logs nothing, so it was extrapolated backwards one 15s interval from the first *failing* heartbeat, and independently bounded by the reaper's own predicate (which at reap time required `last_seen_at < 12:03:18.86Z`); the entry itself says so. The inference is sound and its error is at most a fraction of a heartbeat interval, but it is a derived anchor sitting inside a ~16.6s spread, so a W2-3 author must not treat that spread as three clean measurements. Read that way, all three are inside the contract and the spread is best explained as reaper-tick phase — where the staleness crossing happens to land within the 30s tick. A scenario trying to discriminate "heartbeat staleness at 89s vs 91s" by watching wall-clock reaps would be reading a signal roughly a third the size of its own noise. The spec already lists W2-3 in the Level-1 class (`...design.md:225`); W1's numbers convert that from a preference into a requirement — drive `ListStuckRunIDs` directly with constructed `last_seen_at`/`claimed_at` values, exactly as W0-2 drove `checkAndFireSchedules`, and keep compose only for an end-to-end sanity arm.
  2. **W2-3 must treat orphan-failing as *two* mechanisms with different clocks, and must state which one each arm tests.** W1-5's addendum failed a run through the **heartbeat reconcile** (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:99-124`, `heartbeatReconcileGrace` 60s from `claimed_at`), *not* the stuck-run reaper — and the reason is a boundary behavior W2-3 will hit head-on. Stated with the right causal emphasis: **that partition was 53.0s long against a 90s `staleAfter`, so `ListStuckRunIDs`'s `a.last_seen_at < NOW() - staleAfter` conjunct (`internal/store/postgres.go:1224`) never matched on any tick** — the reaper was never in play, rather than having been kicked out of play by the heal. The 60s claim grace did expire (`claimed_at`+60s = `12:10:18.72Z`) and the reconcile then failed the run at `12:10:31.73Z`, only **13.0s** later — so the grace boundary was not "long since" crossed by the time the run settled; the two events are adjacent, and it is the reconcile's clock, not the reaper's, that produced the outcome. **The inferred generalization (code inference, not a measurement, and not unconditional): a successful heartbeat that keeps `last_seen_at` fresher than `staleAfter` keeps the run out of the reaper's candidate set — but only while the agent row still exists and stays fresh.** The predicate is `(a.id IS NULL OR a.last_seen_at < NOW() - staleAfter)` over a `LEFT JOIN agents`, so once the background sweep `DeleteStaleAgents(ctx, 5*time.Minute)` (`cmd/controller/main.go:390`, `internal/store/postgres.go:1203`) removes the agent row, `a.id IS NULL` satisfies the conjunct on its own and the run becomes reapable again. W1-6 observed that deletion happening (`agent2` gone from `agents` ~5 min after its last heartbeat) though not its reaper consequence. W2-3 should treat the "one heartbeat immunizes the run" rule as holding on a 5-minute horizon, not permanently, and should verify it rather than inherit it. So: any W2-3 arm that intends to test the reaper must hold the agent stale for the *whole* window (revocation, per W1-6, does this unconditionally and is the cleaner instrument; a healable partition does not), and any arm that lets the agent recover is testing the reconcile path instead. The discriminator is cheap and W1 validated it — the reaper logs a `WARN "stuck-run reaper: failed orphaned run (agent lost)"` naming the `runId`, whereas the reconcile path logs nothing and is identified by `runs.updated_at` matching the agent's first post-grace heartbeat (matched to ~30ms in W1-5's addendum).
  3. **The "lock release on reap" limb is already answered end-to-end; downgrade it to a boundary re-check and spend the budget on 1 and 2.** W1-5 confirmed `FinishRun` performs `DELETE FROM mutex_holders` in the same transaction as the status flip (`internal/store/postgres.go:768-770`) and demonstrated the consequence live: a successor acquired the mutex and went Queued-to-Succeeded in **732ms**, with `mutex_holders` and `named_lock_slots` both `(0 rows)` on direct inspection. Because it is one transaction, there is no boundary for a timing scenario to straddle — the interesting residual is not leakage but *physical overlap*, which W1-5 also measured (the successor acquired, ran and finished while the zombie was demonstrably still appending side effects, and the zombie went on appending until its own loop ran out) and which W2-3 cannot improve on without fencing tokens that do not exist. Recommend W2-3 keep a single cheap assertion here and drop the timing arm.
- **(b) Does the nginx-block tooling suffice for W2's partition needs? Yes — W2 does not need a real packet-level partition, and no Dockerfile/`NET_ADMIN` work should be scheduled for it.** W1-5 established the precise scope of the tool: because unified-cd's agent protocol is strictly agent-polls-controller (claim, heartbeat, log push, step/finish report and cancel poll are all agent-initiated; the controller never dials the agent), an nginx per-agent IP deny is **equivalent to a one-way partition in what the agent can accomplish**, and everything W2 keys on — `last_seen_at` ceasing to advance, claims aging past their grace, locks releasing on reap, a replacement agent re-registering under the same ID — depends only on that. It is **not equivalent in what the agent observes**: the agent sees prompt `403`s rather than a TCP black-hole of connect timeouts and half-open sockets, and that single difference is what produced the W1-5 major (a transport error is not an `HTTPError` and would have been retried normally). Checked against each W2 scenario: W2-1, W2-5, W2-6, W2-7, W2-8 and W2-9 need no partition at all (W2-7 in particular is two *live* agents sharing one ID, not an unreachable one); W2-2, W2-3 and W2-4 need only "the agent cannot reach the controller", which nginx-block delivers exactly — and for W2-2 and W2-4 a plain `kill-hard` on the agent is an equally valid instrument. **The one thing W2 must do is not read agent-side error classification, or a terminal status derived from a 4xx, as a result** — under nginx-block those are contaminated by the known artifact, and any W2 run that settles `Failed` via report abandonment should be attributed to the W1-5 major rather than re-filed. Two further notes for whoever eventually does need transport-level failure (a fix-verification pass on the W1-5 major, or a W6 abuse scenario): `test/edgecase/tools/inject.sh` **already ships `partition`/`heal`**, implemented as `docker network disconnect/connect`, which produces DNS/dial failures rather than HTTP status codes and needs no container capability — it is a full disconnect rather than a one-way block, but for a strictly agent-polls-controller protocol the two are indistinguishable in controller-visible state. It was **not exercised anywhere in W1**, so it is unvalidated, and validating it is a cheap prerequisite for that future work — not for W2.

### Tooling and asset notes carried forward

- **`spec.concurrency.mutex` is the only working form — and this branch already got it wrong once, so do not re-derive it from an old snippet.** `test/edgecase/workloads/mutex-successor.payload.json` and `sideeffect.payload.json` were created by this branch with a `spec.mutex` shorthand that does not exist in the schema, and both 400'd. **Both were fixed in `b3158ce` and are correct at HEAD — nothing to do.** They are safe to copy as-is. What a W2/W3 author must not copy is the campaign plan's older worked snippets, which were the source of the mistake; those have also been corrected (`plan:166`, `:176`), but any other pre-`b3158ce` snippet lying around is suspect. `Mutex` lives on the `Concurrency` struct (`internal/dsl/types.go:86`) and `dec.KnownFields(true)` (`internal/dsl/parse.go:94`) makes any stray top-level field a hard 400 rather than an ignored key.
- `kill-soft postgres` does not produce an outage while pooled connections stay open (PostgreSQL Smart Shutdown); the W1-2 runbook now carries a caveat block. Any later wave wanting a clean-shutdown DB fault must use `pg_ctl stop -m immediate`/`SIGQUIT` inside the container, or shorten the pool's idle-connection lifetime.
- **The 26-38s restart-to-`readyz`-200 gap is NOT controller unavailability, and no later wave may budget it as such.** An earlier version of this bullet read "Controller reboot-to-`readyz`-200 varied from ~13s to ~37s across otherwise identical restarts of the same compose file" and turned that variance into a methodological instruction; both the attribution and the instruction were wrong, because the branch's own captured controller logs contradict them.
  - **Measured — controller process boot + leader election is ~1-2s.** `w1/w1-3/controllers.log`: `controller3` `"controller listening"` `05:51:14.950901078Z`, `"scheduler became leader"` `05:51:15.153092142Z` — ~0.2s after the restart window closed (`05:51:15Z`). `w1/w1-4/race-a-controllers.log`: `controller3` listening `06:26:54.274319995Z`, leader `06:26:54.475259384Z` — ~1.2-1.4s after the restart was issued (`06:26:53.111Z`). W1-1 agrees independently: leader re-election at +0.3-2.3s of its `up -d` window and first successful agent report at +3-5s.
  - **Unexplained — the 26-38s residual is rig-side, and its mechanism is not established.** W1-3 saw `readyz` 200 at `05:51:46Z` (~31s past a controller that was leader at `05:51:15.15Z`); W1-4 Race A saw it at `06:27:31.986Z` (~37s past leader at `06:26:54.48Z`); W1-4 Race B saw only ~12-13.5s. **Leading suspect, explicitly NOT established:** `test/ha/nginx.conf` declares its upstreams as `server controllerN:8080`, which nginx resolves **once at config load** — a restarted container that comes up on a new IP would keep being proxied to the stale address until nginx's per-upstream `fail_timeout=5s`/`proxy_next_upstream` machinery works through it, or until nginx is reloaded. That is a hypothesis fitting the shape, not a diagnosis: nothing in this wave tested it, and no nginx-side evidence was captured. **A second, equally live possibility is measurement resolution:** the `readyz` polls were driven by `sleep 10` loops in each runbook and their output was never redirected to the scratchpad, so the true 200 could have arrived up to a full poll interval before it was observed, and coarse sampling cannot be excluded as a partial or total explanation of the variance.
  - **Consequence for later waves — W2-4 in particular.** W2-4 (`...design.md:89`, "Queued-run reaper grace boundary during full agent outage, racing agent recovery at the edge") is designed around exactly this timing, and must **not** treat 30-37s as a window of controller unavailability to race against: the control plane is up and has a leader ~1-2s after restart. Budget recovery from the leader-election log line, not from `readyz`. More generally, `readyz`-flipping-to-`200` remains the right *anchor* for measuring an agent-observed latency (as W1-4 Race A did) precisely because it bounds what a client could see — but it is an upper bound on the LB's observation path, not a measurement of controller readiness, and the two must not be conflated. Any wave that needs the real figure should capture controller logs (cheap, and this wave already proves they answer the question) and should redirect its `readyz` poll output to a file.

## W2-1 — ten of the eleven "leader-elected" background jobs have no leader at all: the advisory lock is a per-tick mutex, so each sweep executes up to N× per nominal interval on N replicas (3 here; 2.15-2.40 measured), on a different replica each time
- **Invariant:** Documented contract (`docs/high-availability.md:75-77`, `:81-90`, `:94`, `:102-105`, `:113-121`, `:382-384`). I1 (run accounting) is cited only to say it **held** — see the observation entry below; nothing was double-executed *concurrently*, which is why this is minor rather than major.
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md`, Phase 2. Plain `test/ha` stack, three controllers, completely idle (no jobs applied, no runs). Postgres statement logging enabled at runtime (`ALTER SYSTEM SET log_statement='all'`, `log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h '`) so every `pg_try_advisory_lock`/`pg_advisory_unlock` call is recorded with its timestamp, backend pid, bind parameter (the lock key) and client container IP. Raw evidence: `w2-1/postgres-full.log.gz`, `w2-1/postgres-idle-window.log.gz`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt`, `w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt`, `w2-1/controller-boot.txt`.
- **Observed:** `RunScheduler` is the only job that behaves the way the docs describe. It acquires `schedulerLockKey` once and holds it for the goroutine's lifetime (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:22`, `:45-56`); across the whole 1555.1s run its holder never changed except at the deliberate failover, and the two non-leaders polled `pg_try_advisory_lock(1702388580)` every 200ms — 7775 attempts from `controller2` and 6713 from `controller3` (`w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt:7`) — of which **exactly one succeeded**: `controller3` at `16:27:26.439`, the deliberate failover (`w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt:38`, `w2-1/failover.txt:34`). `controller2` never acquired it. (An earlier draft of this entry said "0 acquisitions" for both. That was wrong, and it was not a measurement: the analyzer's per-key acquisition tally is built only from paired `try`→`unlock` intervals, and a *sticky* acquisition never unlocks, so the scheduler's key has no interval rows at all and every acquisition of it lands in the analyzer's separate "unreleased" list. See the blind-spot note in the observation entry below.) The other ten call `AcquireAdvisoryLock` at the top of a per-tick helper and `defer release()` before it returns, so **the lock is free again ~1ms later and the next replica to tick simply takes it**. Measured over the 573.4s all-three-replicas idle window (`w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt`), per job:

  | Job | try calls (c1/c2/c3) | successful acquisitions | tick clusters | acquisitions per nominal interval |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Stuck-run reaper (30s) | 20/20/20 | 43 | 20 | 2.15 |
  | Queued-run reaper (30s) | 20/20/20 | 44 | 20 | 2.20 |
  | AppSource sync reaper (30s) | 20/20/20 | 45 | 20 | 2.25 |
  | AppSource reconciler (30s) | 20/20/20 | 45 | 20 | 2.25 |
  | Approval reaper (1m) | 10/10/10 | 24 | 10 | 2.40 |

  **The measured 2.15-2.40 is a floor on this rig, not the model's ceiling — the general prediction is up to N× on N replicas.** Holds are 0.0000-0.0100s against a 30s (or 60s) tick, a duty cycle of ~3×10⁻⁵, so three *independently phased* replicas would essentially never collide and all three would win every cluster. The reason this rig fell short of 3.00 is boot alignment, not contention in any interesting sense: `controller2` and `controller3` booted **0.7ms** apart (`16:04:06.901551791Z` / `16:04:06.900874202Z`, `w2-1/controller-boot.txt:7,12`), so their `time.Ticker`s stay sub-millisecond aligned for the whole run and one of that pair loses every tick, while `controller1` booted ~34ms earlier and therefore always wins its own slot. The Q3 cluster histogram shows exactly that shape: `winners/cluster={3: 5, 2: 15}` for the reconciler over 20 idle clusters (`w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt:69-74`) — i.e. the 3-winner outcome does occur, it is just rare when two of three tickers are phase-locked. A cluster with more jitter between replica start times (a rolling deploy, a k8s rollout, staggered restarts) should approach N. **Later waves budgeting sweep cadence or DB load must use `interval / N`, and must not treat 2.x as the constant.**

  Over the whole run (1555.1s, three replicas for the first ~1343s and two after the failover kill) the same shape holds: stuck-run reaper 149 attempts / **107** acquisitions / 52 clusters, queued-run 149/109/52, sync reaper 149/115/52, reconciler 149/114/52, approval reaper 75/61/26 (`w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt`). The winners are spread across replicas, not concentrated on one — e.g. the AppSource reconciler's 114 acquisitions split controller1=45, controller3=48, controller2=21. One 30s tick, **annotated reconstruction** — not a verbatim excerpt (`w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt:4-8`, key `1937012075` = stuck-run reaper). The raw capture is five `DETAIL:  parameters: $1 = '1937012075'` lines carrying only timestamp, backend pid and client IP; the verb column (`pg_try_advisory_lock` vs `pg_advisory_unlock`) and the `acquired`/`lost` markers below are **reconstructed** from the preceding `LOG: execute stmtcache_…` line of each pair in `postgres-full.log.gz` and from the analyzer's interval pairing. The reconstruction is sound and the timestamps and pids are exact; the word used here was previously "verbatim", which it is not:

  ```
  16:08:36.890 [210] h=172.20.0.3  pg_try_advisory_lock  -> acquired
  16:08:36.891 [210] h=172.20.0.3  pg_advisory_unlock
  16:08:36.923 [215] h=172.20.0.5  pg_try_advisory_lock  -> acquired
  16:08:36.923 [219] h=172.20.0.4  pg_try_advisory_lock  -> lost (never unlocks)
  16:08:36.924 [215] h=172.20.0.5  pg_advisory_unlock
  ```

  i.e. within 34ms the stuck-run sweep ran **twice** — once on controller1 and once on controller3 — and controller2's attempt failed. **Correction to the evidence file's own header:** `w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt:3` says "the 3rd replica (controller1) ticks 167ms later", which contradicts the data directly beneath it. `172.20.0.3` **is** controller1 (`w2-1/baseline-gate.txt`), and it is **first** at `16:08:36.890`, ~33ms *before* controller3 (`172.20.0.5`) and controller2 (`172.20.0.4`) at `16:08:36.923`. There is no 167ms gap and no third-place controller1 anywhere in this cluster; the ordering that matters is controller1 → {controller3 wins, controller2 loses}. The evidence file is raw capture and was not edited, so a later reader of `edgecase-evidence/w2/w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt` will meet that wrong header — this paragraph is the correction of record. The boot ordering is what explains the pairing: `controller2` and `controller3` booted 0.7ms apart (`16:04:06.901551791Z` / `16:04:06.900874202Z`, `w2-1/controller-boot.txt:7,12`), so their tickers are millisecond-aligned and one of the two always loses; controller1 booted ~34ms earlier and therefore always wins its own slot. **No tick cluster ever had zero winners** (minimum winners/cluster = 1 across all 52 clusters of every key), so no sweep was ever skipped.
- **Expected:** `docs/high-availability.md:75-77` — "Jobs that must not run on more than one replica are restricted to a single leader via PostgreSQL **session-level advisory locks**. When the leader goes down, the lock is released and another replica picks it up on the next tick." The table at `:81-90` says "Only the **leader** ..." for each job individually, and `:102-105` states "each background job in the table above elects its own leader independently via its own advisory-lock key, **using the same mechanism described below**" — where "the mechanism described below" (`:113-121`) is explicitly the sticky one: `tick -> Am I the leader? (release != nil) -> YES ... -> NO -> try pg_try_advisory_lock`. Only `RunScheduler` implements that. For the other ten there is no `release != nil` state carried between ticks and therefore **no leader in any durable sense**: the "leader" is whichever replica's ticker fires next, and it changes many times per minute.

  Two further statements in the same document are contradicted more sharply than the ones above, because they assert the sticky model as a property of the implementation rather than as a summary:
  - `docs/high-availability.md:94` — "Advisory locks are held at session level on a dedicated DB connection **for the lifetime of the goroutine**." This is a blockquote attached to the whole arbitration table, i.e. it is asserted of all eleven jobs. It is true of exactly one. For the other ten the measured hold is 0.0000-0.0100s (median 0.0010s) out of a 30s-to-1h goroutine lifetime — the *connection* is indeed dedicated and session-level, but the *lock* is not held for the goroutine's lifetime, and it is the lock that determines whether there is a leader.
  - `docs/high-availability.md:382-384` — "`RunStuckRunReaper`, **leader-elected the same way** as the other background jobs … Every **30s**, **the leader** calls `ListStuckRunIDs(staleAfter=90s, grace=60s)`." Measured: the stuck-run sweep ran **43 times in 573.4s** (every ~13.3s, not every 30s) across **three different replicas** in the same window (controller1=20, controller3=18, controller2=5 — `w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt:21-22`). Both the cadence and the singular "the leader" are wrong as written, and this passage matters more than the table because it is the prose an operator reads when reasoning about reap latency.
- **Notes:** Filed as **minor**, deliberately, and the reasoning matters more than the label. The *safety* property the lock exists for is intact — each job acquires the lock **around** its work and releases only after it (`approval_reaper.go:41-50`, `stuckrun_reaper.go:42-50`, `queuedrun_reaper.go:49-57`, `appsource_sync_reaper.go:44-52`, `appsource_reconciler.go:75-84`), so two replicas can never execute the same sweep concurrently, and the sweeps are set-based/CAS-guarded so a redundant re-run finds an empty candidate set and is a no-op. Nothing was corrupted and no invariant broke. What is wrong is the **documented model**, and it has four practical consequences an operator would get wrong by reading the docs — three of them documentation-only, the third behavioural: (1) **DB load** — each 30s job issues its query ~N times per interval on an N-replica cluster, not once (this is on top of the polling cost measured in the third entry below); (2) **cadence** — the effective sweep interval is roughly `interval / N`, e.g. the stuck-run reaper swept every 13.3s (43 sweeps / 573.4s) against a documented 30s. That *tightens* reap latency rather than loosening it, so it is not a correctness problem, but any latency bound derived from "the leader sweeps every 30s" is conservative by a factor of N and any capacity model derived from it understates query load by the same factor; (3) **cross-tick per-replica state is silently defeated — the one consequence here that is behavioural rather than documentation-only.** Two of the leader-elected jobs carry state that only makes sense if the same replica keeps winning: `internal/controller/archiver.go:25-28` creates `bo := newFailureBackoff(time.Minute, time.Hour, 10_000)` **outside** the tick loop under the comment "**Leader-local**: a failure-backoff instance lives for the lifetime of this goroutine so a run that keeps failing to archive doesn't keep filling every oldest-first batch", and `internal/controller/run_retention.go:35-38` has the identical construct and comment for delete failures. With leadership rotating every tick, the replica that recorded the failure is usually not the replica that runs the next sweep, and the other N-1 replicas have **empty** backoff maps — so the poisoned run is re-selected into the oldest-first batch on the very next sweep and the mitigation is defeated by the same non-stickiness this entry documents. This is the **same defect shape already filed against `RunGitResolver`** (third entry below, item (b)) — there it arises because the job has no lock at all, here because the lock is not sticky; the mechanism differs, the consequence is identical, and the fix for both is the same (hoist the acquire, or move the backoff state into the DB). **Standing: code-read, not demonstrated live.** Both jobs are unwired on `test/ha` (log archiver needs `obj != nil`, run retention needs `UNIFIED_RUN_RETENTION_DAYS > 0`; both boot-log as disabled — `w2-1/controller-boot.txt`), so neither goroutine existed during this run and no backoff behaviour was observed. That is the same standing this entry grants its git-resolver claims. Note also that these two comments are on unexported constructs, so per this campaign's classification rule they are **not** a documented contract and cannot on their own carry a violation — they are cited as evidence of *intent* that the implementation does not deliver, which is why this stays inside a minor entry rather than becoming one of its own; (4) **debuggability** — an operator grepping for "which replica is the leader" will find exactly one answer (`"scheduler became leader"`, the only leadership log line in the system, `scheduler.go:55`) and will reasonably but wrongly conclude that replica is also the one running the reapers. Cheapest fix is documentation: split the table's "Arbitration" column into *sticky leader* (scheduler only) and *per-tick mutex* (the other ten), and correct `:102-105`'s claim that all of them use the scheduler's mechanism. A code fix — hoisting the acquire out of the per-tick helper, as `RunScheduler` does — would make the docs true and cut the query load, but would also lengthen the effective sweep interval back to the documented value and concentrate all background work on one replica, so it is a design decision, not an obvious bug fix. **Also worth correcting in the same pass:** the table at `:79-92` omits two of the thirteen background jobs entirely — the stuck-run reaper (which *is* leader-elected; its arbitration is described in prose at `:380-395` instead) and `DeleteStaleAgents` (which is not; see the third entry below) — so a reader cannot infer an omitted job's arbitration from its absence in either direction.

## W2-1 — observation: advisory-lock mutual exclusion held perfectly on the plain stack — 15,665 lock events with zero overlapping holds on the per-tick keys, a single unshared scheduler holder at every census, and leader failover in 0.40s — which is the negative control the W0-1 pgbouncer split-brain finding needed
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting) — **held**. This entry exists to put the evidence for a negative on the record.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — no violation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md`, Phases 1, 2 and 4. Raw evidence: `w2-1/census.txt`, `w2-1/census-postfailover.txt`, `w2-1/failover.txt`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-postfailover.txt`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt`, `w2-1/baseline-gate.txt`, `w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt`.
- **Observed:**
  - **The `objid` -> key mapping was verified empirically, not assumed.** The W2 plan's mapping (`pg_locks.objid` = the low 32 bits of the advisory key) is standard Postgres semantics but is not read from this repository, so it was pinned against the one key whose holder can be independently attributed. Every census row for `objid = 1702388580` (`0x65786364`, `schedulerLockKey`) read `classid = 0`, `objsubid = 1`, `granted = t`, `client_addr = 172.20.0.3` (`w2-1/census.txt`), and `172.20.0.3` is `controller1` (`w2-1/baseline-gate.txt`), which is the container — the only container — that logged `"scheduler became leader"`, at `16:04:07.070468889Z` (`w2-1/controller-boot.txt`). Two independent identifications of the same holder agree, so the mapping and the `pg_stat_activity` attribution method are both confirmed. After the failover the same check re-passed against the new leader (`objid 1702388580`, pid 216, `client_addr 172.20.0.5` = `controller3`, which logged `"scheduler became leader"` at `16:27:26.439510266Z`).
  - **No key was ever held by two replicas at once — but two different instruments carry the two halves of that claim, and neither covers both.** Stating which is which matters, because the entry is a negative control and a negative control is only as good as its coverage.
    - **The ten per-tick keys: the statement log.** This is the complete record rather than a sample: **15,665 advisory-lock events** were parsed across the 1555.1s run, hold intervals were reconstructed by pairing each acquisition with the next release on the same backend pid, and the overlap check across pids on the same key found **0 overlaps** — in the idle window, in the post-failover window, and over the whole run (`w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt`, `-postfailover.txt`, `-full.txt`, each ending `overlaps found: 0`). Hold durations were 0.0000-0.0100s, median 0.0010s. **Scope limit — the overlap check is structurally blind to sticky holds and therefore cannot see the scheduler at all.** The analyzer builds hold intervals only from `try`→`unlock` pairs on one pid (`analyze.py:102-114`) and the overlap scan iterates that interval list (`:130-141`); a *sticky* acquisition never unlocks, so it produces no interval, lands instead in the analyzer's "unreleased" list, and **can never be reported as an overlap no matter what happens**. The scheduler's key has zero interval rows for the whole run. So "0 overlaps across 15,665 events" is a strong negative for the **ten per-tick keys** (in practice the five wired-and-ticking ones) and says **nothing whatever** about the scheduler's key.
    - **The scheduler's key: the census plus the leadership log line.** This is the half that answers W0-1, and it is carried entirely by the sampled instrument. Every `pg_locks` census returned exactly **one** row for `objid = 1702388580` — never two, never a second holder — across three samples at 30s spacing pre-failover, one immediately pre-kill, and four at 45s spacing post-failover (`w2-1/census.txt`, `w2-1/failover.txt`, `w2-1/census-postfailover.txt`), plus ten single-key probes at ~3.5s spacing in the 33s after the kill, all reading `pid=216 granted=true client=172.20.0.5/32` (`w2-1/failover.txt:13-32`). Corroborated by the application log: **exactly two** `"scheduler became leader"` lines exist in the entire run — `controller1` at boot and `controller3` at the deliberate failover — where W0-1's split-brain signature was three, from three replicas, with no intervening kill. Because a sticky lock is held continuously, a 30-45s sample is adequate here in a way it is not for a 1ms hold. It remains a **sample**, not a complete record, and that is the correct standing to give it.
    - **Scope correction on the census, for a reader who later reuses these captures.** Only **four** of the eight censuses actually dump every advisory lock in the database: the three in `w2-1/census.txt` and the pre-kill sample in `w2-1/failover.txt:1-6`, each of which runs an unconstrained `... FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype='advisory'` and returns `(1 row)`. The four post-failover samples (`w2-1/census-postfailover.txt`) `LEFT JOIN` a hard-coded list of the eleven known keys, so what they establish is "**one of eleven** keys held", not "one advisory lock in the entire database" — an unexpected twelfth key, or a lock taken by something outside the controller, would be invisible to them. An earlier draft of this entry claimed the stronger scope for all eight. Nothing observed suggests a twelfth key exists; the point is only that these four samples could not have shown one.
  - **Failover was clean and fast.** `docker compose kill -s SIGKILL controller1` (the leader) was issued at `16:27:26.043Z`; `controller3` logged `"scheduler became leader"` at `16:27:26.4395Z` — **0.40s**, and the statement log shows why it is bounded by the 200ms scheduler tick rather than by anything else: the survivors' polls at `16:27:26.039` and `16:27:26.239` both failed and the poll at `16:27:26.439` succeeded (`w2-1/evidence-excerpts.txt`). Postgres released the dead session's lock on its own; `SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE client_addr='172.20.0.3'` read **0** at all four post-failover samples, and no key was left orphaned or doubly held.
- **Expected:** Exactly this. `pg_try_advisory_lock` is a Postgres primitive and `AcquireAdvisoryLock` (`internal/store/postgres.go:1425-1450`) uses it correctly, on a dedicated connection from a separate `lockPool`, with acquire and release on the same physical connection.
- **Notes:** The reason this null result is worth an entry is **W0-1**. That scenario found all three controllers holding "the same" leadership simultaneously under a PgBouncer transaction-pooling overlay, with no error logged anywhere, and attributed it to the pooler multiplexing several logical connections onto a shared server session. This scenario is the control for that claim: the identical stack, identical code and identical keys, with the pooler removed. **Match the instrument to the half of the control it actually supports** (see the scope note in Observed). W0-1's symptom was specifically *the scheduler's* key appearing to be held by three replicas at once, and the statement-log overlap count does not speak to that key at all — it is blind to sticky holds by construction. The scheduler half of the control rests on the census (one holder for `1702388580` at every sample, and `client_addr` pinning it to a single container) plus the fact that only **two** `"scheduler became leader"` lines exist in the whole run, both accounted for by boot and by the deliberate kill, against W0-1's three-from-three-replicas with no kill. The **15,665 events / 0 overlaps** figure is the control for the *ten per-tick keys*, and is the stronger of the two because it is a complete record rather than a sample. Together they isolate the W0-1 defect to the pooling layer and rule out a fault in `AcquireAdvisoryLock` itself — something W0-1 asserted but could not demonstrate, since it never ran the un-pooled comparison. Anyone re-using this control for a *sticky*-lock claim must add an instrument that can see sticky holds: the census, `pg_stat_activity` attribution, or an analyzer change that treats an unmatched `try` as an interval open to the end of the window. **Method note for later waves:** ten of the eleven jobs are silent both when idle and when not leader, so nothing about their exclusion is observable from application logs; runtime Postgres statement logging (`ALTER SYSTEM SET log_statement='all'` + `log_line_prefix='%m [%p] h=%h '`, reverted in teardown) turns the whole question into a complete, timestamped, per-replica record and cost nothing but log volume (~40MB uncompressed over 26 minutes on an idle three-replica stack). It is the right instrument for any later scenario that needs to prove a negative about which replica did what. **Two caveats travel with the reduction script (`w2-1/analyze.py`), both written up in full under "Two traps in the reduction script" in `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md`: its statement matcher sees only the first physical line of a statement and consequently mislabels one output row (see the third entry below), and its overlap check is blind to sticky holds (see Observed above). The lock counts it produces are sound; those two outputs are not. Read the traps section before reusing it.**

## W2-1 — observation: all three unlocked background jobs confirmed running on every replica simultaneously; two are documented as deliberate, `DeleteStaleAgents` is documented nowhere, and the git resolver costs 5.0 queries/s per replica on a completely idle stack
- **Invariant:** none violated. Recorded as risk plus a documentation gap.
- **Severity:** minor (observation)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md`, Phase 3. Raw evidence: `w2-1/unlocked-jobs-per-host-final.txt`, `w2-1/deletestaleagents-statements.txt`, `w2-1/deletestaleagents.txt`, `w2-1/stale-agent-timeline-hardkill.txt`, `w2-1/agent2-hardkill.txt`, `w2-1/stale-agent-timeline.txt`, `w2-1/postgres-full.log.gz`.
- **Observed:**
  - **Concurrent execution is measured, not inferred.** Per-replica statement counts over the 573.4s three-replica idle window (`w2-1/unlocked-jobs-per-host-final.txt`), keyed by client IP (`172.20.0.3/4/5` = `controller1/2/3`): `SELECT id, spec, created_at FROM runs ...` (`ListPendingRuns`, the git resolver's only statement) **2871 / 2871 / 2871**; `DELETE FROM agents WHERE last_seen_at < NOW() - $1::interval` (`DeleteStaleAgents`) **10 / 10 / 10**; `DELETE FROM oidc_states WHERE expires_at <= NOW()` **1 / 1 / 1**. Exactly equal counts from all three replicas, matching the 200ms / 1m / 10m tick intervals.

    > **Do not read the analyzer's `ListPendingRuns(git resolver)` row instead of this one — that row is mislabelled and reports a different statement.** `w2-1/lock-analysis-idle.txt:79` says `ListPendingRuns(git resolver) controller1=2867 controller2=0 controller3=0` and `w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt:71` says `6712 / 0 / 1063`, both flatly contradicting the 2871/2871/2871 above. **The numbers in this entry are the correct ones** (`w2-1/unlocked-jobs-per-host-final.txt:4-7`, a direct `grep | sort | uniq -c` of the statement log by client IP); the analyzer row is counting `TransitionPendingToQueued`, i.e. the **scheduler's leader-only** Pending snapshot. Cause: `analyze.py:54` requires `"status = 'Pending'"`, `FROM runs` and `ORDER BY created_at` to appear on the **same logged line**, and it only ever sees the first physical line of a statement because its line regex demands the `%m [%p] h=%h` prefix. `TransitionPendingToQueued`'s SQL is a single line (`internal/store/postgres.go:440`, `SELECT id, spec, params FROM runs WHERE status = 'Pending' ORDER BY created_at LIMIT $1`) and matches; `ListPendingRuns`' SQL is three lines (`postgres.go:2066-2068`) whose first line is just `SELECT id, spec, created_at FROM runs` and matches nothing. The distribution is the tell and confirms the diagnosis exactly: the mislabelled row tracks **leadership**, not replica count — `2867/0/0` in the idle window when `controller1` held the scheduler lock throughout, and `6712/0/1063` over the whole run, where 6712 is `controller1` up to the failover kill and 1063 is `controller3` after it (`controller3` became leader at `16:27:26.439`). A per-replica job cannot produce a column of zeroes. The near-match between 2867 and 2871 is coincidence with a cause: `RunScheduler` and `RunGitResolver` are both 200ms tickers in the same process, so their statement counts track each other. After the leader was killed the same statements continued from the two survivors at the same per-replica rate (1060 / 1060 `ListPendingRuns` over 211.8s). This is the direct evidence the W2 plan said could not be obtained: it does not rely on the `deleted=N` log line at all.
  - **The `DeleteStaleAgents` race, resolved.** `agent2` was SIGKILLed at `16:17:56.19Z` with `last_seen_at = 16:17:50.461548+00`, so it became eligible at `+5m = 16:22:50.46`. The row was still present at the `16:22:49.07Z` sample and gone at the `16:23:19.66Z` sample (`w2-1/stale-agent-timeline-hardkill.txt`). Exactly **one** controller logged the deletion — `controller1`, `{"time":"2026-07-29T16:23:06.996697513Z","msg":"deleteStaleAgents","deleted":1}`, and `grep` across all three controllers returns that single line (`w2-1/deletestaleagents.txt`). The statement log shows what actually happened underneath: **three** unsynchronised `DELETE`s in that one minute, `16:23:06.980` (controller1), `16:23:07.012` (controller3), `16:23:07.013` (controller2) — a 33ms spread — of which the first deleted the row and the other two matched nothing and therefore logged nothing (`w2-1/deletestaleagents-statements.txt`). The plan's stated limitation ("a single deleted row cannot distinguish one replica ran from three raced and two saw N=0") is real and is exactly why the log line alone is insufficient; it is the statement log, not the application log, that discriminates them.
  - **What the docs say, which is where the plan's premise fails.** `docs/high-availability.md:91-92` lists **Git resolver** and **OIDC state cleanup** in the arbitration table with `none (idempotent)` / "Runs on all replicas", so for those two the absence of a lock **is** the documented design and this observation confirms the docs rather than contradicting them. The W2 plan's Recording rule ("absence of leader election on the three unlocked jobs = **violation**") is therefore wrong for two of the three, and no violation is filed for them. `DeleteStaleAgents` is the third, and it is **absent from that table and from every other statement about arbitration in `docs/`** — `:374` and `:443` describe its 5-minute threshold and its interaction with the stuck-run reaper but never say whether it is leader-elected. That is a gap rather than a contradiction, which is why it is an observation and not a violation; the operational cost is small (an idempotent `DELETE`, N-1 of which are no-ops) but a reader cannot determine the answer without reading `cmd/controller/main.go:382-398`.
  - **The git resolver's polling cost is measured; its duplication cost is not.** 2871 queries per replica over 573.4s is **5.006 queries/s per replica**, i.e. **15.0/s** across three replicas, on a stack with zero jobs and zero runs — the 200ms ticker at `cmd/controller/main.go:444` fires unconditionally and `resolveGitPendingRuns` (`internal/controller/scheduler.go:290-291`) queries before it knows there is nothing to do. This scales as `5N/s`.
- **Expected:** For the git resolver and the OIDC cleanup, exactly what `docs/high-availability.md:91-92` describes. For `DeleteStaleAgents`, nothing is documented either way.
- **Notes:** **The interesting question — whether the docs' "harmless if duplicated" claim about the git resolver is true — was NOT answered live, and no claim is made that it was.** Demonstrating it needs a git-backed job fixture (a git-over-HTTP server container); this campaign has none, and W1-7 was deferred for the same reason. What can be stated is the code-read shape, with three specific reasons the "harmless" claim deserves testing rather than trust, **all three inferred from source and none observed**: (a) `resolveGitPendingRuns` does a full `resolver.ResolveSpec` — a git fetch plus, for private repos, a secret decrypt (`scheduler.go:300-326`) — per candidate per replica, so the remote git host and the key-manager path see N times the traffic, and "idempotent" says nothing about that cost; (b) the `failureBackoff` that exists precisely so "a poisoned candidate ... doesn't fill every batch" (`scheduler.go:286-288`) is created **inside the goroutine** (`scheduler.go:274`) and is therefore per-replica, so a candidate excluded on replica A is still selected by B and C and the mitigation is defeated by the very concurrency the docs bless — note that this comment is on an unexported helper and so, per this campaign's classification rule, is **not** a documented contract and cannot on its own carry a violation; (c) the resolve-deadline path (`scheduler.go:338-348`) calls `AppendLog(... systemLogStepIndex ...)` **before** `MarkRunFinished` with no lock and no compare-and-set, so N replicas reaching the deadline in the same tick can each append the same operator-visible `git template resolution failed for more than ...` line to the run's log — a duplicated side effect on the one surface an operator reads. (a) and (c) are the ones worth a scenario. **Carry-forward: this needs its own scenario in W3 or later, gated on the git-over-HTTP fixture, and should be folded into whatever work builds that fixture rather than standing alone.** Two further notes for later waves. First, `ListPendingRuns(ctx, 50, ...)` is the *same* 50-row `ORDER BY created_at` snapshot that W2-9 is about, taken independently on every replica — so the head-of-line story there has an N-replica multiplier on its query cost, though not on its semantics. Second, **`docker compose stop` is the wrong instrument for aging out an agent**: a healthy agent's SIGTERM path deregisters itself (`internal/agent/agent.go:349`), issuing `DELETE FROM agents WHERE id = $1` and logging `"agent deregistered"`; the first attempt here saw `agent2`'s row vanish **0.9s** after `stop` (`16:07:05.014Z` stop, `16:07:05.319` DELETE, row absent at the `16:07:05.943Z` sample — `w2-1/stale-agent-timeline.txt`), never exercising `DeleteStaleAgents` at all. The probe was redone with `kill -s SIGKILL`. That deregistration behaviour is undocumented in `docs/` (`grep -rn -i deregister docs/` matches nothing) but is not filed as a finding here — it is recorded so the next scenario does not lose an hour to it.

## W2-1 — observation: five of the eleven advisory-lock keys are unobservable on the `test/ha` stack, for two different reasons, and neither is a defect
- **Invariant:** none. Recorded so a later reader does not mistake an empty census row for a dead job.
- **Severity:** minor (observation — null result)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-1-leader-exclusion.md`, Baseline gate + Phase 1. Raw evidence: `w2-1/controller-boot.txt`, `w2-1/baseline-gate.txt`, `w2-1/census.txt`, `w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt`.
- **Observed:** Across the whole 1555.1s run, five keys recorded **zero** `pg_try_advisory_lock` calls from any replica (`w2-1/lock-analysis-full.txt`, "keys with ZERO acquisition attempts"): log archiver (`0x6C6F6761`), cache cleanup (`0x63616368`), audit retention (`0x61756474`), run retention (`0x7272746E`), log trim (`0x6C74726D`). Two distinct causes, both confirmed from the controllers' own boot logs (`w2-1/controller-boot.txt`, identical on all three replicas):
  - **Four goroutines were never started.** `test/ha/docker-compose.ha.yaml` sets no `UNIFIED_S3_*` and no `UNIFIED_DATA_DIR`, so `obj == nil` (`cmd/controller/main.go:303-322`) — boot log `WARN "no object store configured — log archival disabled"` — and the two `obj != nil`-gated goroutines at `main.go:399-402` (log archiver, cache cleanup) are never launched. It sets no `UNIFIED_RUN_RETENTION_DAYS` / `UNIFIED_LOG_TRIM_DAYS`, both of which default to 0 (`main.go:47-58`, `:64-75`), so `RunRunRetention` (`run_retention.go:29`) and `RunLogTrim` (`log_trim.go:29`) return before creating their tickers — boot logs `"run retention disabled (keep forever)"` and `"log trim disabled (DB log rows kept forever)"`.
  - **The fifth is wired but its first tick had not arrived, and this contradicts the W2 plan.** The plan lists audit retention as needing its flag, alongside run retention. It does not: `auditRetentionDaysDefault()` (`main.go:26-41`) falls back to **90 days** when `UNIFIED_AUDIT_RETENTION_DAYS` is unset, and all three controllers logged `INFO "audit log retention enabled" retentionDays=90` at boot. `RunAuditRetention` therefore *is* running here — but on a 1-hour ticker (`main.go:420`) whose first fire is at boot+1h, i.e. `~17:04Z` against a stack booted `16:04Z` and torn down at `16:32Z`. Its exclusion behaviour is **unobserved**, not absent, and no claim is made about it in either direction.
- **Expected:** A key with no holder is only a defect if the job is both wired and past its first tick. The plan's Recording rule ("a wired job with no key holder across all samples = major") needs that third condition; without it, a 1-hour job on a 26-minute observation would be filed as a major defect.
- **Notes:** The practical instruction for any later scenario reusing this census: check the boot log first. Four of the five absences here are visible as explicit `disabled` lines, the fifth as an `enabled` line whose interval exceeds the observation window. **A key with zero holders is three different results — not wired, wired but not yet ticked, and wired but broken — and only the third is a finding.** Audit retention is the one job of the eleven whose leader exclusion this campaign has not exercised at all; if it matters, it needs either a much longer soak or a direct `runAuditRetentionOnce` probe under the `edgeprobe` build tag rather than a compose scenario.

## W2-2 — a cascade-cancelled `call:` child, and every plainly-cancelled run, is left with a step permanently reporting `Running` under a terminal run
- **Invariant:** I7 (state display consistency) violated; I1 (run accounting) held throughout — the run settles on exactly one terminal state, its locks are released, and the defect is that a row *describing* the run contradicts it. W1-5 filed the identical defect under I7 (`:330`), and this file's own rule at `:306` says a run that settles on exactly one terminal state which is merely *wrong* is "an I7 concern, not an I1 one".
- **Severity:** minor
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-2-agent-replacement.md`, Part A and the two no-fault controls. Raw evidence: `w2-2/partA-step-reports.txt`, `w2-2/partA2-cancel-control.txt`, `w2-2/partA3-cancel-simple-run.txt`, `w2-2/final-sweep.txt`.
- **Observed:** In Part A, `docker compose restart agent1` made the controller fail the parent `10c7834e` (`updated_at 17:12:42.889561`) and cascade-cancel its linked child `503a3d6e` (`17:12:42.892803`, 3.2 ms later). Both runs are correctly terminal. But the child's only step stayed `Running` with a NULL `ended_at`, both in the mid-scenario capture (`w2-2/partA-step-reports.txt`, which carries no timestamp header of its own) and at the timestamped final sweep `17:27:28.931Z` — **14m46s after the run went `Cancelled`** (`w2-2/final-sweep.txt:20`):

  ```
   503a3d6e-5295-4057-81aa-8c4cbe17af76 | 0 | emit | Running |
  ```

  Two no-fault controls show this is **not** specific to agent replacement:
  - **Plain `POST /runs/{id}/cancel` on a `call:` parent** (`w2-2/partA2-cancel-control.txt`): cancel at `17:18:28.171Z` → parent `Cancelled 17:18:28.214176`, child `Cancelled 17:18:28.217776`, and **both** the parent's own `invoke_child` step and the child's `emit` step left `Running/end=NULL` through the last sample at `17:19:35.579Z` and still at the final sweep.
  - **Plain cancel of an ordinary single-step run** with no `call:` at all (`w2-2/partA3-cancel-simple-run.txt`): `edge-longrun` cancelled at `17:20:15.903Z` (204), run `Cancelled 17:20:15.938076`, step `0:tick=Running/end=NULL` at every sample to `17:20:58.808Z` and at the final sweep. It is visible through the public API, not just in the DB:

    ```
    GET /api/v1/runs/52a7be75-.../steps
    [{"index":0,"stageIndex":0,"name":"tick","status":"Running","startedAt":"2026-07-29T17:20:09.432208Z","kind":"run","section":"main"}]
    GET /api/v1/runs/52a7be75-...
    {"...","status":"Cancelled","claimedBy":"agent1"}
    ```

  The mechanism is a two-sided gap, both sides confirmed from source. (a) Neither `handleCancelRun` (`internal/controller/api_runs.go:377-384`) nor `cancelDescendantRuns` (`:390-412`) calls `MarkRunStepsInterrupted`; only `failOrphanedRun` (`internal/controller/stuckrun_reaper.go:76-90`) does, and only for the run it is failing — which is exactly why the three runs terminalized by the reconcile path in this scenario (`10c7834e:1`, `998f3dc3:0`, `1897dd94:0`) all *do* carry an `ended_at` in `w2-2/final-sweep.txt`. (b) The agent's own late terminal report cannot fix it: agent2 was told to stop at `17:12:45.877601Z` (`w2-2/partA-agent2.log`, `"received cancellation signal from master; interrupting run"`) and POSTed `/api/v1/agents/agent2/steps` at `17:12:46.882986Z` — **HTTP 200** — but `handleAgentStepReport`'s already-finalized guard (`internal/controller/api_agent.go:511-523`) sees the run is `Cancelled` and returns `200 {"alreadyFinalized":true}` **without writing the row**. So the report is accepted, discarded, and the agent has no way to know.
- **Expected:** **The project has already adjudicated this behavior as a defect, and shipped a fix for it.** PR #113 (`2c018fe`, "fix(controller): terminalize in-flight steps when reaping an orphaned run") is merged on `main`; its commit message states the symptom in the project's own words — the UI "showed a Failed run with a step stuck on `Running` even though nothing was executing (observed live: a long-running job whose macOS agent went offline mid-step)". So this entry is **not** filed on the absence of a documented contract. It is filed on the fact that **a shipped fix covers one of `MarkRunFinished`'s callers and misses the rest — including, decisively, the remainder of the very path it targeted.** `failOrphanedRun` (`internal/controller/stuckrun_reaper.go:76-90`) is `MarkRunStepsInterrupted(runID)` → `MarkRunFinished(runID)` → `cancelDescendantRuns(runID)`: the step terminalization is scoped to *its own* `runID` (`:81`), while the descendants that same function cascade-cancels three lines later (`:88`) are terminalized through the **bare** `MarkRunFinished` at `api_runs.go:406`. Part A's child `503a3d6e` is therefore a **reap-path** victim — a casualty of PR #113's own code path — not a cancel-path one. That is exactly why the three runs the reconcile terminalized *directly* in this scenario (`10c7834e:1`, `998f3dc3:0`, `1897dd94:0`) all do carry an `ended_at`, and the one it reached only via the cascade does not. The residual surface is far wider than the cancel endpoint: `cancelDescendantRuns` has **four direct call sites** — `api_runs.go:382` (cancel endpoint), `api_agent.go:626` (agent's own parent-run-finished report), `queuedrun_reaper.go:77`, `stuckrun_reaper.go:88` — and is reached from **seven entry points** once `failOrphanedRun`'s four callers are expanded (stuck-run reaper `stuckrun_reaper.go:58`, heartbeat reconcile `api_agent.go:114`, startup reconcile `:824`, claim-build failure `:188`). Every one of them terminalizes descendants without touching their steps. (The W2 plan's facts block listed five "call sites" for `cancelDescendantRuns`; those were `failOrphanedRun`'s callers plus the queued-run reaper, and the list omitted both the cancel endpoint and the agent parent-finish report — corrected in the plan.) `docs/high-availability.md:413-416` promises `call:` descendants are "cascade-cancelled" and says nothing about step rows, so the *docs* are silent; the authority here is PR #113 plus the W1-5 entry at `:330`, not the docs.
- **Notes:** Nothing re-drives it: `ListStuckRunIDs` selects `status='Running'` runs only, so a terminal run's dangling step is never revisited. Severity stays **minor**, on this file's own rubric and deliberately: run-level accounting is correct in every case (the run is terminal, locks are released, no work is lost or duplicated), and `:306` states the campaign's rule that a run settling on exactly one terminal state which is merely wrong is "an I7 concern, not an I1 one" — W1-5 was accepted at minor on identical reasoning. What keeps it from being negligible is that the visible symptom is **wrong, not merely absent**, and that it fires for **every cancelled run in the system**. `web/src/routes/RunDetail.svelte:648-658`'s `stepDuration()` does `const end = s.endedAt ? new Date(s.endedAt).getTime() : Date.now();` (`:651`), so a step with a NULL `ended_at` renders a **live-incrementing elapsed time**: a run that terminated hours ago shows its step still counting up, on a reactive component that re-renders on poll. **Fix — defer to W1-5's proposal, which is the better one.** The W1-5 entry (`:330`, Notes at `:347`) already located the correct site: do the step terminalization inside `FinishRun`'s existing transaction (`internal/store/postgres.go:746-780`) — `UPDATE step_reports SET status = <run status>, ended_at = NOW() WHERE run_id = $1 AND status = 'Running'` — which covers every `MarkRunFinished` caller at once and adds no round trip, since the transaction and the per-run call already exist for lock release. The narrower alternative this entry would otherwise propose — call `MarkRunStepsInterrupted` from `handleCancelRun` and from `cancelDescendantRuns`' per-child branch — closes the two paths this scenario exercised but leaves the hole open at every future `MarkRunFinished` call site, which is precisely the way PR #113 came to be incomplete. Prefer the funnel.

## W2-2 — observation: the startup reconcile has no claim grace and failed a run 2.409s after it was claimed, while both other orphan definitions carry a 60s grace
- **Invariant:** I1 (run accounting)
- **Severity:** observation (as-designed; documented)
- **Repro:** `test/edgecase/scenarios/w2-2-agent-replacement.md`, Part B. Raw evidence: `w2-2/partB-poll.txt`, `w2-2/partB-docker-events.txt`, `w2-2/partC-mutex.txt`, `w2-2/final-sweep.txt`.
- **Observed:** `edge-longrun` `998f3dc3` was claimed by `agent1` at `claimed_at 17:24:48.599292`. `docker compose restart agent1` was issued **0.495 s later** at `17:24:49.094Z`. The replacement process registered at `17:24:51.004587Z`, its reconcile call made `controller2` log `"agent reconcile: failed orphaned run (agent process replaced)" runId=998f3dc3... agentId=agent1` at `17:24:51.010864Z`, and the run's terminalization landed at `updated_at 17:24:51.007957`. Postgres' own arithmetic (`w2-2/partB-poll.txt`, final DB view):

  ```
   status | claimed_at                    | updated_at                    | age_at_terminal
   Failed | 2026-07-29 17:24:48.599292+00 | 2026-07-29 17:24:51.007957+00 | 00:00:02.408665
  ```

  Part C reproduced it independently at **4.050 s** (`1897dd94`, claimed `17:26:30.978435`, Failed `17:26:35.028818` — `w2-2/partC-mutex.txt`), and Part A at 98.073 s. Both other orphan definitions would have refused all three: the heartbeat path's `ListReconcilableRunIDsByAgent` (`internal/store/postgres.go:287-309`) adds `claimed_at < NOW() - make_interval(secs => $2)` at `:294` (`heartbeatReconcileGrace = 60s` at `internal/controller/api_agent.go:71`) and the stuck-run reaper carries the same 60 s `grace` (`cmd/controller/main.go:404`). The startup path's `ListRunningRunIDsByAgent` (`internal/store/postgres.go:266-285`) is a bare `SELECT id FROM runs WHERE claimed_by = $1 AND status = 'Running'` (`:271`) with no time predicate at all. **Cite these two carefully — an earlier draft of this entry gave `:294-297` for `ListRunningRunIDsByAgent`, which is inside the *sibling* and points at the graced query, i.e. the exact opposite of the point being made.**
- **Expected:** This is exactly what `docs/high-availability.md:414-415` promises — "the controller fails every `Running` run still claimed by that agent ID" — with no time qualifier, and `:416-417` explains why the call is retried until it succeeds ("claiming with unreconciled orphans would leave the hole open"). **Recorded as an observation, not a violation**, because the documented contract and the code agree.
- **Notes:** The operational cost is worth stating plainly even though the behavior is correct: because the reconcile is unconditional and `Failed` runs are never re-queued (`docs/high-availability.md:401-404`), restarting an agent destroys whatever it holds *at that instant* regardless of how recently the work started — and a fleet-wide rolling restart therefore fails every in-flight run in the fleet.
